* * * * 


[155] within the range of reasonableness in light of the best 
possible recovery. The settlement must be rejected. 

There were minimal risks attendant trial of the class 
action representatives’ cases. This has been acknowledged 
by the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee. They have 
consistently taken the position that the cases would have 
been won at the trial level. Victor Yannacone and Benton 
Musslewhite confirm the absence of risks attendant trial. 
Only with respect to an appeal have members of the 
Plaintiffs’ Management Committee expressed concem. What 
the Court of Appeals might do, however, is not a basis upon 
which this Court may accept an inadequate, unreasonable and 
unfair settlement. 

In sum, a review of the factors set forth in Grinnell l call 
for the Court to reconsider its approval of the $180 million 
settlement and ‘o find that settlement to be unfair, 
unreasonable and inadequate. Additional considerations call 
for the Court to vitiate the settlement. 


Il. 
In Addition to the Grinnell Factors, There Are 


A Number of Other Reasons to Vitiate The Settlement 
A. The Class Action Representatives 

May Have Been Inadequate __ 

No class representative represented class members who 
were deceased or who had not manifested injury. All of the 
class representatives were living and had manifested injury. 
The [156] interest of those who had died or not yet 


eee 


145 


manifested injury were not protected. Indeed, the interests of 
living class representatives who had manifested injury were 
antagonistic to the interests of those who had died and to 
those who had not yet manifested injury. The more money 
set aside for those who had died and for those who had not 
yet manifested injury, the less money available to those who 
are living and who have manifested injury. 


In fact, the interests of those who have died may have 
been sacrificed to obtain a settlement for the living class 
members. In seeking a 23 (b) (1) (B) certification, the class 
action representatives stated to the Court that the wrongful 
death and survival claims alone would amount to $10 billion. 
See, Special Master’s Report Re: Applicability of 23 (b) 
(1)(B), Docket No. 1562a at 7. The settlement was for only 
$180 million. A question is clearly raised as to whether the 
claims of those who had died were sacrificed to obtain a 
settlement for the living class members. 


Special Master Feinberg’s distribution scheme suggests 
that the interests of those who have died are still being 
sacrificed for the interests of those who are living. Under 
Special Master Feinberg’s proposal, wrongful death and 
survival claims will be limited to $5,000 dollars to be paid 
out over 10 years while those living class members with 
cancer may receive a maximum of $25,000. See, Exhibit 9. 
Indeed, the class action [157] representatives may not have 
even adequately protected their own interests much less the 
interests of those class members they represented. 


According to three class action representatives, the class 
action representatives were not consulted about demands, or 
counter offers during the settlement negotiations but rather 
were requested to approve a fait accompli. Prior to becoming 
class representatives, David Lambiotte and Danny and 
Christine Ford were Ashcraft & Gerel’s clients. After the 
settlement negotiations, all three class representatives 
advised Robert A. Taylor, Jr., a member of the firm of 
Ashcraft & Gerel, that they had not been consulted during the 


146 


course of settlement negotiations and were simply asked to 
approve the settlement once completed. See, Exhibit 1. 


In approving this settlement, the Court has an obligation 
to be sure that the class representatives were adequate 
representatives of the class as originally certified and as 
enlarged by the settlement agreement. Malchman v. Davis, 
706 F.2d 426, 432 (2nd Cir. 1983). 


Not only must the court examine whether plaintiffs 

had interests antagonistic to those of other class 

members (citations omitted), but also whether the 

class representative ‘possess[ed] the same interest 

and suffer[ed] the same injury’ as the class members 

(citations omitted). 

Id. at 433. As set forth infra, the class definition in the Legal 
Notice of Pendancy of Class Action was altered by the [158] 
Settlement Agreement to include the claims of those who had 
not yet manifested injury. All of the class representatives 
had manifested injury. Not one of the class representatives 
was an adequate representative of those class members who 
had not yet manifested injury. . 

The Court must also take care to assure that there is no 
release of claims not shared by all members of the class, e.g. 
wrongful death claims, which represents an advantage to the 
class by the uncompensated sacrifice of those claims. TBK 
Partners, Ltd. v. Western Union Corp., 675 F.2d 456, 461 
(2nd Cir. 1982). The class action sgn sh 
inadequate representatives of those class mem 
ae ok and survival claims. They had not suffered 
those injuries. Furthermore, there may have been an 
uncompensated sacrifice of the wrongful death and survival 
claims to advantage the class. The future claims of those 
who had not yet manifested injury may have been sacrificed 
in order to obtain a settlement for those class members who 
had manifested injury. Finally, the class action 
representatives were totally excluded from the settlement 
negotiations. They could not, therefore, protect their own, 


147 


much less class members’ interest. The Court must inquire 
into these matters or permit discovery by counsel prior to any 
finding that the settlement is fair, reasonable and adequate. 


[159] 


B. Statements Under Oath By Benton Musslewhite 
And Victor J. Yannacone, Jr. And Certain 
Memoranda Released By Victor J. Yannacone, Jr. 


Indicate That Class Action Counsel May Have 
Been Inadequate 


A statement under oath by Victor J. Yannacone, Jr., a 
copy of which is found as Exhibit 21, and certain memoranda 
released by him, copies of which are found as Exhibit 11, 
raise serious questions as to the adequacy of class action 
counsel. Class action counsel must have been adequate 
representatives of the class from the beginning to the end of 
the litigation. Any breach in adequacy may have materially 
affected the litigation and adversely impacted on settlement. 
According to Mr. Yannacone, class action counsel were 
clearly inadequate to the task of protecting class members’ 
interests from shortly after the organization of Yannacone & 
Associates (hereafter, also the Consortium) to approximately 
May, 1983 when he removed himself from the Consortium. 


Mr. Yannacone's statement under oath paints a 
distressing picture of small Long Island firms lacking the 
finances, manpower and desire to be class action counsel. 
Unless otherwise indicated, the following information was 
obtained from Statement Under Oath by Victor Yannacone, 
Jr., Exhibit 21. Page references are to Mr. Yannacone's 
Statement. Ashcraft & Gerel does not vouch for the truth or 
veracity of Mr. Yannacone's statements but only suggests 
that his statements under oath raise serious questions as to 
the adequacy of counsel [160] and as to the consequent 
fairness, reasonableness and adequacy of the settlement. 


Yannacone & Associates was established in September, 
1979 (p. 12). The Consortium consisted primarily of small 
firms (pp. 31, 38-39). A number of the members did 


148 


principally defense work in personal injury cases (pp. 31-32). 
Other than Mr. Yannacone, none of the members of the 
Consortium had experience in large product liability or mass 
toxic tort litigation (p. 43). Due to the size of their offices 
and the press of other practice sustaining litigation, each of 
the member firms assigned only one attorney on a part-time 
basis (p. 31). No law clerks or paralegals were assigned to 
the case (p. 39). 


Among the firms in the Consortium, only one high speed 
copier existed (pp. 37-38). That copier was in Mr. 
Yannacone's possession. All the other firms had only small 
convenience copiers (p. 38). At various stages, only a few 
firms in the Consortium had any word processing capability 
(pp. 40-41). An attempt at computerization was made but 
aborted for financial reasons (pp. 39-40, 42). 


From the inception of Yannacone & Associates to 
approximately April, 1983, members of the Consortium 
refused to review microfilmed documents produced by the 
government and defendants (pp. 34-36). The government 
began producing documents on microfilm in April of 1981. 
By April of 1983, over [161] 283 rolls of microfilm had been 
produced by the government. Mr. Yannacone purchased 
some of the rolls in July of 1982 (p. 34). Mr. Keith 
Kavanaugh and Mr. Yannacone were forced to read 
approximately 140 rolls without any help from other 
members of the Consortium (pp. 34-36). Requests by Mr. 
Yannacone for help from Consortium members went 
unheeded (p. 37). The documents produced by the chemical 
companies went unreviewed with limited exception (pp. 35, 
41). According to Mr. Yannacone, "[a] decision was made . . 
. that no attention would be paid to causation, and no 
attention would be paid to preparing any document search 
even in the area of liability" (p. 34). Apparently, a decision 
was also made “largely on the basis of costs" that no 
depositions would be taken by plaintiffs until defendants had 
completed their deposition discovery (p. 59). 


149 


When motions for summary judgment were made on the 
government contract defense in April of 1983, Yannacone & 
Associates was unprepared to respond (p. 69-69A). Dow, 
Hercules, Thompson Hayward (now T.H. Agriculture & 
Nutrition Company), Uniroyal and Thompson Chemical 
Company moved for summary judgment. Ashcraft & Gerel, 
which had become a member of a newly formed Plaintiffs’ 
Management Committee only a month before, was called 
upon to write the oppositions to three out of the five motions 
for summary judgment (pp. 68-69A). Ashcraft & Gerel was 
originally asked to write the oppositions to Dow's and 
Thompson Hayward's motions for summary judgment. 
Having [162] completed that task, Robert A. Taylor, Jr. of 
Ashcraft & Gerel was asked the day before it was due to also 
write the opposition to Thompson Chemical Company's 
motion for summary judgment. He did so (pp. 68-69A). 
Ashcraft & Gerel also provided all the documents necessary 
for a member of Yannacone & Associates to write the 
Opposition to Uniroyal's motion for summary. See, Affidavit 
of Robert A. Taylor, Jr., Exhibit 1. Only the oppositions to 
Hercules' and Uniroyal's motion for summary judgment was 
not written by Ashcraft & Gerel. Jd. Summary judgment 
was granted to defendant-Hercules by the Honorable George 
C. Pratt. See, Affidavit of Robert A. Taylor, Jr. for an 
explanation as to why Ashcraft & Gerel as non MDL lead 
counsel or class counsel from the beginning of the litigation 
to March, 1983 was prepared to respond to defendants’ 
motions for summary judgment approximately two months 
after joining the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee. 


By May of 1983, when Mr. Yannacone removed himself 
from Yannacone & Associates, between 150-200 depositions 
had been taken by defendants (pp. 54, 58-59). Numerous 
requests by Mr. Yannacone prior to May of 1983 for aid in 
reviewing the depositions met with no response from the 
other members of Yannacone & Associates (p. 54). 


Apparently, the primary problem was finances. There 
were insufficient finances to pay for deposition transcripts; 


150 


telephones; computers and computer services; clerical 
personnel and computer key operators; travel; copying; 
microfilm machines [163] and the miscellaneous expenses 
associated with litigation (pp. 34, 37-38; 40, 42; 51-53; 57- 
59). Importantly, a refusal by the Consortium to commit 
time and resources made it impossible to move for partial 
summary judgment on elements one and two of the 
government contract defense (p. 66). A lack of adequate 
finances appears to have also been at the root of major 
personality clashes within Yannacone & Associates which 
paralized that group. (Musslewhite Statement, p. 10). 


Mr. Yannacone withdrew from Yannacone & Associates 
in May of 1983. In late 1983, Yannacone & Associates 
asked permission to be relieved as MDL lead counsel and 
class action counsel. Yannacone & Associates cited financial 
difficulties as the reason for the request. After being 
thwarted in an attempt to gain control over the litigation, 
Ashcraft & Gerel withdrew as a member of the Plaintiffs’ 
Management Committee. See, Affidavit of Robert A. 
Taylor, Jr., Exhibit 1, for an accounting of that withdrawal 
and related matters. 


A number of documents released to the news media by 
Victor J. Yannacone, Jr. (which served in part as the basis for 
an article by The American Lawyer in April of 1984) call 
into question the adequacy of MDL and class action counsel. 
A number of those documents were provided to Ashcraft & 
Gerel by Mr. Yannacone after he had furnished copies to the 
media. Some of those documents are to be found as Exhibit 
11. A copy of The American Lawyer article is also to be 
found as Exhibit 12. A [164] number of Mr. Yannacone's 
contemporaneous documents corroborate his statements 
under oath. Mr. Yannacone's statements under oath and his 
contemporaneous documents raise serious questions as to the 
adequacy of class action and MDL lead counsel from the 
inception of the litigation to May of 1983. The inadequacy 
which Mr. Yannacone alleges to have existed apparently had 
an impact on the settlement as seen from Mr. Benton

Mr. Musslewhite first became involved in the Agent

in representing a large number of Vietnam veterans (pp. 5-6

MDL lead counsel and Mr. Musslewhite anticipated entering 
into a monitoring Status (p. 8). With time, however, Mr. 
Musslewhite came to recognize that Yannacone & Associates 
may not have been adequately protecting his clients (p. 8-9). 
Yannacone & Associates was not purchasing copies of 
microfilmed documents produced by the government and 
was not taking depositions (Statement Under Oath By Victor

In February of 1983, Yannacone & Associates was in 
such financial troubles and spread so thin that it invited Mr. 
Musslewhite and Steven Schlegel to become members of a 
new Plaintiffs’ Management Committee. Ashcraft & Gere! 


152 


became a member in March of 1983. From February of 1983 
to September of 1983, Mr. Musslewhite witnessed the further 
degeneration of Yannacone & Associates through bitter 
feuding and financial strangulation (p. 14). 


When Ashcraft & Gerel and then Yannacone & 
Associates withdrew from the Plaintiffs’ Management 
Committee, only Steven Schlegel, Benton Musslewhite and 
Thomas Henderson, who had been brought aboard only a 
month earlier, remained. They did not [166] have the 
finances or manpower necessary to continue the litigation. 
Money and manpower was obtained by entering into a fee 
sharing and expense reimbursement scheme with six (6) 
other law firms. 

The Plaintiffs’ Management Committee's internal 
expense reimbursement and fee sharing arrangement raises 
serious questions as to the adequacy of representation 
afforded by class counsel. A copy of that agreement is found 
as Exhibit 13. On December 7, 1983, Stephen J. Schlegel, 
Thomas W. Henderson and Benton Musslewhite, who had 
been appointed as the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee, 
entered into an agreement with six other attorneys to obtain 
additional manpower and financing. The agreement was 
executed only five (5) months before trial was scheduled. 
Under the terms of the agreement, those who advanced costs 
of the litigation were to receive three (3) times the amount of 
money they advanced, prior to the distribution of ariy other 
monies. The remainder of the award was to be divided as 
follows: 50% in equal shares among all committee members, 
30% in proportion to hours worked, and 20% based on 
factors paralleling those considered by Courts in granting 
fees. "The result would be that those who advanced money 
would be advantaged to an extraordinary degree over those 
who gave their time and skill to the enterprise. 
Memorandum and Order on Attorney Fees And ba 
Judgment, January 7, 1985, p. 60. Under the terms f 
agreement, the new “financiers” controlled a [167] majority 
of the votes. Upon information and belief, only after 


153 


settlement negotiations were completed and the case settled 
did the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee divulge to the 
Court its internal arrangement. 


Upon learning of the agreement, the Court was clearly 
concerned 


At the hearing on fees, the court indicated that 
the then-current internal management agreement 
was unlikely to withstand close scrutiny. Members 
of the PMC subsequently entered into a new 
arrangement permitting reimbursement of moneys 
advanced by a multiple of three and allocation of the 


rest of the fee awards to those who were awarded 
them by the court. 


Memorandum and Order on Attorney Fees and Final 
Judgment, p. 63. As the Court properly noted in its 
Memorandum "“[a] division of fees without regard to work 
performed or responsibility assumed is not ‘in compliance 
with the standard’ for fixing fees. Prandini v. National Tea 
Co., 557 F.2d 1015, 1019 (3rd Cir. 1979). The ‘touchstone 
for the fee [is] to be the actual effort made by the attorney to 
benefit the class.' Grinnell II, 560 F.2d at 1099." Id., at 61 
(parenthetical in original). See also, ABA Model Rules of 
Professional Conduct, Code DR 5-103(A). 

By May 7, 1984, the day of trial, five (5) of the nine (9) 
members (a majority) had contributed $200,000 each to the 
Plaintiffs’ Management Committee. Each of the five (5) 
stood to make $600,000 from a settlement on May 7, 1984. 
On that date, [168] the five (5) financiers’ 300% return on 
investment was without risk. Interestingly, each of the five 
(5), financiers became involved in the litigation only six 
months before settlement. 

Mr. Musslewhite's Statement Under Oath appears to 
indicate that an ability of a majority of the Plaintiff's 
Management Committee to obtain a 300% return on their 
investment and receive a share of fees for work performed by 


154 


others had an adverse impact on settlement negotiations. Mr. 
Musslewhite has stated under oath: 


I can tell you from statements made to me by 
Schwartz, he wanted to take the money and run 
because Newton is always that way. That is one 
reason I had difficulty sometimes in dealing with 
Newton in that is the way he is. He doesn't care 
whether you try a case and win or what. As soon as 
he thinks a settlement is reasonable and his estimate 
of what is reasonable is so far below mine, they 
don't even come close. So Newton is a settling 
lawyer. He doesn't like to try cases and he doesn't 
try any cases to speak of. He just settles and runs. 
(pp. 100-101)

Mr. Musslewhite's statements under oath clearly suggest that 
the Plaintiffs’ Management Committees’ internal expense 
reimbursement and fee sharing arrangement adversely 
affected settlement. Even if the agreement had no effect on 
the settlement, the appearance of impropriety is so great as to 
require the Court to vitiate the settlement. 

In his statement, Mr. Musslewhite makes known that 
Stanley Chesley, Gene Locks and Thomas Henderson had no 
individual clients (p. 99). Newton Schwartz and Robert 


— 


155 


O’'Quinn only obtained clients or, more appropriately, 
obtained an interest in the recoveries of certain clients 
through an agreement to provide financing to Mr. 
Musslewhite (p. 99). Once Messrs. Chesley, Locks, 
O'Quinn, Schwartz and Henderson became members of the 
Plaintiffs’ Management Committee and were appointed as 
class counsel, they had a fiduciary duty to all members of the 
class. The absence of individual clients was thereafter of no 
legal consequence. Nonetheless, a lack of individual clients 
may have adversely affected Mr. Chesley's ability to 
adequately represent ihe class in settlement negotiations, with 
which he was principally charged." In the absence of 
previously representing individual Vietnam Veterans, their 
spouses and children, in the absence of experiencing first 
hand the egregious injuries nobly suffered by those clients, 
the class members and their injuries may have been mere 
abstractions [170] to Mr. Chesley. Clearly, zealous 
representation is fostered by a desire to remedy an injustice 
to persons known by name, face, and who are known to have 
suffered severe and unjust injury. Stanley Chesley's lack of 
individual clients before becoming class counsel may have 
had a detrimental impact on his ability to represent the class 
during settlement negotiations. 

Indeed, Mr. Chesley indicated that he could not try the 
case because of his active involvement in the Bendectin 
litigation (p. 65). Mr. Chesley was far too removed from the 
Vietnam Veterans, their families and their litigation to have 
been chosen to engage in settlement negotiations. 

Mr. Musslewhite's statement under oath further indicates 
that Mr. Chesley's involvement in the Bendectin litigation 
may also have resulted in Mr. Chesley failing to adequately 
inform himself of the strengths and weaknesses of the class 
representatives’ cases for the purpose of settlement 
negotiations (p. 74-77). Like Agent Orange, the Bendectin 
litigation is a major MDL/class action. Mr. Chesley played a 
leading role in bringing about a settlement in that litigation 
which was ultimately overturned on appeal. Like the Agent 


156 


Orange litigation, preparation to try the Bendectin case was a 
24 hour job. Query, whether Mr. Chesley had sufficient time 
to prepare the Bendectin case for trial and adequately inform 
himself of the strengths and weaknesses of the class 
representatives’ cases in the Agent orange litigation. 

[171] The agreement making Mr. Chesley a member of 
the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee is dated December 7, 
1983 --- only five months before trial was set. Prior to Mr. 
Chesley becoming a member of the Plaintiffs’ Management 
Committee, over three (3) years of pretrial proceedings and 
discovery had occurred. There was clearly insufficient time 
for Mr. Chesley to acquaint himself with that discovery and 
those proceedings. Indeed, Mr. Musslewhite's statement 
under th indicates that it may have been impossible for Mr. 
Chesley to inform himself of past discovery. According to 
Mr. Musslewhite, half of the depositions taken in the 
litigation remained unsynopsized (p. 50). Finally, evidence 
supporting the inference in Mr. Musslewhite's statement that 
Mr. Chesley failed to fully inform himself of the strengths 


and weaknesses of the class representatives’ cases is found in 


Mr. Musslewhite's statement that Mr. Chesley never sat 
down with him to discuss the strengths and weaknesses of 
the government contract defense (pp. 76-78). 


Mr. Musslewhite was chiefly in charge of defeating the 
government contract defense at trial. Had Mr. Chesley 
discussed the defense with Mr. Musslewhite, he would have 
assured Mr. Chesley that the defense posed little obstacle to a 
favorable jury verdict (p. 106, 110). Query whether Mr. 
Chesley negotiated under a misapprehension as to the 
strength of that defense. To the extent the spectre of that 
defense was relied upon at the fairness hearings to justify a 
clearly inadequate settlement, the possibility of a 
misapprehension on the part of Mr. Chesley is not remote. 
See, Fairness Hearings.

Apparently, Mr. Chesley failed in the responsibility he 
assumed to determine the number of potential claims which 
might be filed against any settlement fund. Two examples 
attest to this failure: Mr. Chesley did not check with Mr. 
Yannacone, who [174] represented approximately 8,000 
Vietnam Veterans to determine the number and nature of 
those claims and Mr. Chesley did not check with Robert A. 
Taylor, Jr. to determine the number or nature of the claims by 
the 1,000 Vietnam Veterans he represented. See, Exhibit 1, 
Affidavit of Robert A. Taylor, Jr. Had Mr. Chesley checked 
with Mr. Yannacone or Mr. Taylor, he clearly would have 
know that there were far more than 20,000 potential claims 
and that far more than 3,000 to 4,000 serious cases existed. 


As of February 28, 1985, the Agent Orange Computer 
Center had counted 216,000 timely claim forms. Another 
5,000 timely forms are expected. In the 214,000 claim forms 
analyzed, 9,704 cancers, 35,995 miscarriages and stillbirths 
and 65,037 families with one or more children with birth 
defects were identified. No figures were available for the 
number of wrongful death claims, the liver dysfunctions, 
renal dysfunctions, central and peripheral neuropathies, 
gastro-intestinal disorders, immune deficiencies and related 
problems, chloracnes and the psychosomatic problems, to 
name only a few of the maladies caused by exposure to 
Agent Orange. Obviously the 216,000+ claim forms 
received as of February 20, 1985 does not take into account 
the claim forms to be filed over the next twenty-five (25) 
years by those who manifest injury after February 2, 1985. 


They too are entitled to compensation from the settlement 
fund. 


Indeed, it is questionable whether those charged with 
negotiations had the opportunity to assess the number of 
claims that might be made over the next twenty-five (25) 


160 


years. As discussed, infra, the class definition was altered in 
the settlement agreement during settlement negotiations to 
include [175] for the first time those who had not manifested 
injury. Undoubtedly, those engaged in_ settlement 
discussions had no opportunity to consult biostatisticians to 
determine what might be the expected rate of various 
diseases in the future given present rates of appearance. 


Without an accurate assessment of the number and 
nature of present and future claims, the Plaintiffs’ 
Management Committee could not have adequately 
represented the interests of the class. The failure to take 
steps necessary to make an accurate assessment of the 
number and nature of present and future claims is fatal to the 
settlement. The Court may not approve a class action 
settlement if representation has been inadequate. 


Mr. Musslewhite’s statement under oath attests to further 
serious inadequacies of representation. According to Mr. 
Musslewhite, the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee failed 
to computerize the documents and depositions (pp. 49-52). 
Indeed, Mr. Musslewhite has stated under oath that on the 
day of trial only one-half of over 200 depositions had been 
synopsized (pp. 50, 55). A review of defendants’ Proposed 
Pretrial Orders in conjunction with a list of depositions taken 
brings to light that on the day trial was to have begun, only 
26 of the 81 live medical causation witnesses to be called by 
the defendants had been deposed. In total, defendants listed 
160 live causation witnesses they intended to call at trial. In 
separate proposed pretrial orders, defendants were to list their 
live liability witnesses. Settlement removed that obligation. 
It is unknown how many of defendants’ liability witnesses 
were not deposed. 

[176] According the Mr. Musslewhite, documents 
remained unorganized for purposes of preparing 26(b) 
statements and, inferentially, for trial. Eight of plaintiffs’ 14 
liability witnesses were excluded as a result of Mr. 
Musslewhite's failure to submit complete 26(b) statements (p.

Ashcraft & Gerel received a call from a member of the 
Plaintiffs’ Management Committee three weeks before the 
May 7, 1984 trial date, asking the firm to assign Robert A. 
Taylor, Jr. to work full-time on the Government Contract 
Defense and requesting partners James Green and Robert 
Adams to devote as much time as was possible to defeat that 
defense at the upcoming trial. Though Ashcraft & Gerel had 
withdrawn from the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee in 
September of 1983, the request seemed urgent enough, and 
troubling enough in light of the doctrine of stare decisis, to 
cause the firm to begin a shifting of responsibilities to 


provide the requested aid. Settlement, of course, terminated 
those activities. 


Apparently, the call for help was made by Mr. 
Musslewhite without authorization by the Plaintiffs’ 
Management Committee. When he advised them of his 
request, the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee voted him 
down. Why the Plaintiffs’ Management [177] Committee 
refused sorely needed help is unclear. Was it a fear that 
Ashcraft & Gerel would learn the level of preparation to go 
to trial (p. 46)? The very fact of the call raises a question as 
to the level of preparedness of the Plaintiffs' Management 
Committee. Indeed, the extremely low settlement figure 
raises this same question.

C. Serious Defects in the Class Action 

Call For The Settlement to Be Set Aside 

1. The Class Definition Is Imprecise 

The requirement that there be a class will not be deemed 
satisfied unless the description of it is sufficiently definite so 
that it is feasible for the court to determine whether a 
particular individual is a member. The class definition must 
not be amorphous or imprecise. Consequently, whether a 
person is a member of a class may not depend on his state of 
mind or subjective belief. Ihrke v. Northern States Power 
Company, 459 F.2d 566, 573 (8th Cir. 1972), Jimeniz v. 
Weinberger, 523 F.2d 689, 698 (7th Cir. 1975); cf., In Re 
Agent Orange Product Liability Litigation, 100 F.R.D. 718, 
728-29 (2nd Cir. 1983). The definition of the class in this 
case, requires the Court to ascertain a person’s state of mind 
to determine whether that [185] person is a class member. 
The court has to determine whether a person believes that 
Agent Orange caused his or her injuries. 

The Clerk of Court’s letter which prefaces the March 19, 
1984 Legal Notice To Class Members of Pendancy of Class 
Action stated: “This lawsuit is a class action on behalf of 
persons . . . who allege injury.” The class also includes 
spouses, parents and children . . . who claim direct or 
derivative injury . . . (Emphasis added). The actual notice 
stated: 

2. The plaintiffs include spouses, parents, and children 
born before January 1, 1984, of the service persons 
who claim direct or derivative injury as a result of 
exposure. 

In Re Agent Orange Product Liability Litigation, 100 F.R.D. 
at 731-32 (emphasis added). Similarly, the radio and 
television notice was styled to catch the attention of those 
who “claim injury.” Jd. at 734. The newspaper and 


mae 


169 


magazine notice highlights the state of mind problem. 

notice stated: om 
If you or anyone in your family can claim injury, 
illness, disease, death or birth defect as a result of 
exposure to Agent Orange. . . 

Id., at 734-35. 


Whether one claims (believes) or can claim (capable 
believing) that his or her direct or derivative ooie rut 
from exposure to Agent Orange is a state of mind. To 
determine whether one claimed or could have claimed injury 
as a result of Agent Orange at or before final judgment 
requires the ascertainment of the person’s state of mind. The 
class definition, hence, is impermissibly imprecise. 

[186] 2. The Class Definition Was Improperl 

Altered After Notice Had Been Sent : 
To the Original Class Memt 

The class was originally defined in the March 9, 1984 
Legal Notice To Class Members of the Pendency of a Class 
Action as set out in the proceeding section. After the notice 
had been sent, the Court accepted a settlement agreement 
which altered the class definition. The altered definition 
brought into the class for the first time persons who had not 
manifested injury and children born after January 1, 1984. 
Page 9 paragraph 8 of the settlement agreement states: “The 
Class specifically includes persons who have not manifested 
injury.” The original notice failed to advise the original 
“claiming injury” class members that “noninjury claiming” 
members and children born after January 1, 1984 would be 
added to the class. Having permitted such an alteration, the 
Court failed to provide proper notice, a renewed opportunity 
to opt-out and to assure that the settlement fund fairly and 
adequately provided for the new claims. Weinberger v. 
Kendrick, 698 F.2d 61, 70, 77 (2nd Cir. 1982). 

3. Notice Was Not The Best Notice Practical Under 

The Circumstances Including Individual Notice 
To All Members Who Could Have Been Identified 


170 
Through A Reasonable Effort 


The fourteenth Amendment guarantees all class 
members due process of law. An opportunity to be heard is a 
fundamental [187] requisite of due process of law. “This 
right to be heard has little reality or worth unless one is 
informed that the matter is pending and can choose for 
himself whether to appear or default, acquiesce or contest.” 
Mullane, Special Guardian v. Central Hanover Bank & Trust 
Co., 339 U.S. 306, 314 (1950). Consequently, a notice 
informing absent class members of the pendency of a class 
action must be reasonably calculated, under all the 
ci) cumstances, to apprise them of the pendency of the class 
action and permit them sufficient time to respond. Id. 

The means employed must be such as one desirous 

of actually informing the absentee might reasonably 

adopt to accomplish it. The reasonableness and 

hence the constitutional validity of any chosen 
method may be defended on the ground that it is in 

itself reasonably certain to inform those affected . . 

Acitations omitted) or, where conditions do not 

reasonably permit such notice, that the form chosen 

is not substantially less likely to bring home notice 

than other of the feasible and customary substitutes. 
Mullane, 339 U.S. at 306. Under the teachings of Mullane, 
written actual notice must be sent to all class members 
known to the class representatives and to those who can be 
identified “with due diligence.” Jd. at 317. Mullane also 
suggests that publication notice may be utilized for class 
members who cannot be identified through a reasonable 
effort if it is of a “form ...not substantially less likely to bring 
home notice than other of the feasible and customary 
substitutes.” Mullane, at 306. The Supreme Court has made 
clear, however, that “‘[nJotice by [188] publication is a poor 
and sometimes a hopeless substitute for actual service of 
notice,’ and that ‘[ijts justification is difficult at best.’” 
Schroeder v. City of New York, 371 U.S. 208, 213 (1962). 


171 


Rule 23 (c) (2) of the Fed. R. of Civ. Pro. codifies these 
constitutional imperatives. 

By its terms, Rule 23(c)(2) is applicable to this 23 (b) (3) 
class action. Rule 23(c)(2) states 

In any class action maintained under subdivision (b) 

(3), the court shall direct to the members of the class 

the best notice practicable under the circumstances, 

including individual notice to all members who can 

be identified through reasonable effort. The notice 

shall advise each member that (A) the court will 

exclude him from the class if he so requests by a 

specified date; (B) the judgment, whether favorable 

or not, will include all members who do not request 

exclusion; and (C) any member who does not 

request exclusion may if he desires enter an 

appearance through his counsel. 
Rule 23(c)(2) of Fed. R. of Civ. Pro. The Supreme Court has 
declared that subdivision (c) (2) expresses an “unambiguous 
requirement” that “individual notice must be provided to 
those class members who are identifiable through reasonable 
effort.” Eisen v. Carlisle & Jacquelin, 417 U.S. 159, 175-76 
(1974). (hereafter Eisen). If publication notice is to be 
utilized for those class members who cannot be identified 
through a reasonable effort, publication of the notice must be 
the “best practical under all the circumstances” and “such as 
one desirous of actually informing the absentee might 
reasonably adopt to accomplish it.” Rule 23 (c)(2); Mullane, 
339 U.S. at 315. 

[189] As set forth infra, the District Court failed to direct 
and the Plaintiffs’ Management Committee failed to give the 
best notice practical under the circumstances. 

a. The Court Failed To Direct And The Class 
Action Representatives Failed To Undertake 
An Identificatica Of Class Members Who 
Could Have Been Identified Through A 
Reasonable Effor_

and addresses of class members. The sources and the

process is detailed in an affidavit dated January 29, 1981 by

addresses of class members were:

some updated addresses (24 persons working for 
one month could identify practically all Vietnam 
veterans who served in Vietnam.

Computerized records maintained at Department of

through a reasonable effort. Of the above sources, only the

Veterans Administration’s Agent Orange Registry with its

[191] A review of some or all of the above listed sources 
for names and addresses was clearly a reasonable effort. The 
class representatives conceded in the Fourth Amended 
Verified Complaint that it was possible to identify each class 
member by reference to records maintained in the ordinary 
course of operations. See, Exhibit 6 to Docket No. 1517. As 
attested to by Mr. Matthew’s affidavit, many of the names 
and addresses are computerized. See, Exhibit 14. 
Furthermore, as a party, the government could have been 
ordered to conduct the computerized and manual searches 
with an entitlement to payment for expenses from the class 
representatives. Oppenheimer Fund, Inc. v. Sanders, 437 
U.S. 340, 356-59 (1974). 


Clearly, the effort to identify the names and last known 
addresses of class members from some, if not all, of the 
above sources was a reasonable effort. 


[O]nce a 23(b)(3) action has been certified, the 
name and last known address of each class member 
known to the parties or capable of being identified 
from business or public records available to them 
must be produced (note omitted). The source or 
sources providing the greatest number of names and 
addresses must be used. (emphasis added). 


In Re: Nissan Motor Corp. Antitrust Litigation, 552 F.2d at 
1088, 1098-1100. As in Nissan, this Court should turn to 
Eisen v. Carlisle & Jacquelin, 417 U.S. 156 (1974) to 
determine the reasonableness of identifying class members 
from the sources listed above. In Eisen, the Supreme Court 
upheld a district court’s finding that, based upon the parties 
access to computerized records, the names and addresses of 
2.2 million class members could be identified through a 
reasonable effort. [192] Eisen, 417 U.S. at 175. Relying on 
Eisen and the dictates of Mullane and Schroeder, the Court in 


175 


Nissan found an admittedly “‘herculean task’” of manually 
identifying the names and last known addresses of class 
members from 1.7 million dealer registration cards to be a 
reasonable effort in light of the size of the class action. The 
Court stated, “/A] large class requires a large effort.” Nissan 
at 1100. (Emphasis added). The Agent Orange class dwarfs 
the class in Eisen and Nissan. The effort in identifying class 
members in the Agent Orange case must dwarf the 
“herculean task” required in Nissan. 


The class representatives should have obtained the 
names and last known addresses of all class members whose 
identity could have been gleaned from computerized records. 
Sources 1, 5, 6, 7, and 8 set out above contain computerized 
records. It was the existence of computerized records which, 
in part, caused the Supreme Court to decide in Eisen that the 
a of 2.2 million class members was a reasonable 
effort. 


Computers have not always been with us and the 
requirement of a manual search does not make the 
identification effort unreasonable. The Court in Nissan 
required the manual search of 1.7 million dealer cards to 
identify class members. Nissan at 1096, 1100. Manual 
searches would be required of sources 2, 4, 6, 7 and 8 above. 
Only a search of source 4 comes close to properly being 
characterized as a “herculean task.” As the Matthews’ 
affidavit accurately points out, however, 24 individuals over 
one months time could complete the task at an estimated cost 
of $30,000. This is a relatively small sum and the Supreme 
Court in Eisen teaches: 


[193] There is nothing in Rule 23 to suggest that the 
notice requirements can be tailored to fit the 
pocketbooks of particular plaintiffs. 
Eisen, 417 U.S. at 176. In addition to failing to use available 
computerized military and governmental records to identify 


class members, the class representatives failed to use even 
available Court records. 


176 


The class representatives should have reviewed the 
caption of each line complaint in MDL 381 to obtain the 
names and addresses contained thereon. No affidavit attests 
to that having been done. The class representatives should 
have reviewed the motions to intervene in Dowd v. Dow, 79 
C 479 to obtain the names and addresses contained therein. 
No affidavit was located in the record attesting to such an 
effort. The class representatives should have contacted 
counsel for the class members who had filed complaints and 
requested the names and addresses of their clients. No 
affidavit attesting to such an effort was located in the record. 
Ashcraft & Gerel provided to the PMC the names and 
addresses of its clients shortly after becoming a member of 
the PMC in March of 1983. From March of 1983 to April of 
1984 when notice issued, the PMC never contacted Ashcraft 
& Gerel to obtain an updated list. During that time, 
additional clients retained Ashcraft & Gerel and old clients 
changed addresses. 


The class representatives clearly failed to identify class 
members who could have been identified through a 
reasonable effort. 


[194] b. The Class Representatives Failed To Provide 
Written 
Notice To Some Class Members for Whom They 


Had A Last Known Address_ 

Even more egregious than failing to obtain from the 
government machine retrievable names and last known 
addresses, was the failure to give notice to those whose 
names and addresses had been furnished to them. For 
example, the state of Massachusetts sent the class 
representatives a computer tape containing the names and 
addresses of 157,496 Vietnam veterans. Letter, Docket No. 
2535. The class representatives did not send notice to that 
group. That fact does not appear of record but was conveyed 
in a telephone conversation to Robert A. Taylor, Jr., Esq. by 
Robert Laquori, who headed the computer mailing. See, 


177 


Exhibit 1. The reason given was the arrival of the tape 
approximately one week before the opt-out deadline of May 
1, 1984. Id. A letter from the PMC acknowledging receipt 
of the computer tapes on April 17, 1984 appears to belie that 
explanation. See, Letter, April 18, 1984, Docket No. 2535. 


Similarly, the class representatives failed to provide 
individual notice to 20,000 West Virginia Vietnam veterans 
for whom they had addressed labels. The State of West 
Virginia provided the class representatives 28,000 mailing 
labels to be used to provide West Virginia’s Vietnam 
veterans with notice of the class action. As explained by 
Robert Laquori in a letter of July 11, 1984 to Chuck Conroy, 
West Virginia’s Agent Orange Assistance Coordinator, only 
8,000 of the 28,000 labels were used. See, Exhibit 15. All 
that had to be done was stuff the envelopes and put on the 
labels. The fact that it was not done [195] is indicative of the 
absence of a desire on the part of the class representatives to 
give the best practical notice, including individual notice to 
those known or identifiable through a reasonable effort. 

Ms. Jan Levin, an attorney with the PMC advised Robert 
A. Taylor, Jr., a member of the law firm of Ashcraft & Gerel 
that only 187,600 written notices were sent. See, Exhibit 1. 
The class is estimated to contain 2.4 million Vietnam 
veterans, their spouses and children. The class could well be 
in excess of 5 million members. Ms. Jan Levin further 
advised Robert A. Taylor, Jr. that out of the 187,600 notices 
mailed, 57,096 were returned as _ undelivered or 
undeliverable. Jd. Roughly one-third of all the written 
notices mailed were not received by class members. Two 
explanations were given by Robert Laquori. First, the 
program he wrote was not 100% efficient in switching the 
information in the “fields” on the Agent Orange Registry 
computer tape to the “fields” on his tape. As he explained, 
the result was that “some of the addresses looked funny.” Jd. 
Another tape was sent from the State of Wisconsin with 
36,389 names on it. According to Mr. Laquori, he believes 
the names and addresses were bogus and there was a 


178 


“conspiracy.” Jd. 30,603 letters addressed using the 
Wisconsin computer tape were returned as undelivered or 
undeliverable. 


The experience of the Australian and New Zealand class 
members is particularly sad and has done little for good 
relations between the United States and the veterans of those 
countries. The veterans of Australia and New Zealand did 
not receive the Legal Notice To Class Members of Pendancy 
of Class [196] Action until shortly before and frequently after 
the May 1, 1984 opt-out deadline. See, Transcript of 
Fairness Hearings in San Francisco. 


Standing alone, the class representatives’ failure to give 
individual written notice to those known to them is fatal to 
the class action. When that fact is combined with a failure to 
undertake reasonable efforts to identify class members and 
such a poor record of mailing notice, there can be no question 
but that the class action must be decertified. 

C. Written Notice W iv 

Even if the written notice reached a class member, the 
notice itself was defective. The written notice failed to 
identify the representative plaintiffs. Representative parties 
are normally identified in a class action notice. Newberg on 
Class Actions § 24.75, p. 147. Identification of the 
representatives allows the class members to determine 
whether their particular situation is represented and their 
interests therefor protected. In this case, the plaintiffs 
asserting survival and wrongful death claims and those who 
had not manifested injury had no class representatives and 
were not advised of that fact. The lack of representation 
permitted their claims to be compromised. See, supra. 


The absence of an_ identification of the class 
representatives and a lack of specification as to the extent of 
their representation also caused plaintiffs’ counsel great 
difficulty in advising their clients. See, Exhibit 16. Letter 
from Ronald L. Wolf, Esq. to Honorable Jack B. Weinstein 
dated [197] March 23, 1984, Docket No. 2299. Counsel 


179 


could not advise their clients whether their interests were 
protected by any of the class action representatives. 

The notice failed to advise certain types of class 
members how to opt-out. The notice failed to advise spouses 
and children of the need to sign separate “Exclusion Request 
Forms.” Perhaps for that reason an estimated 80% of the 
“Exclusion Request Forms” are signed only by the veterans 
and not by the spouses and children. While there may be 
some exceptions, the norm of family life suggests that 
members of a family prefer to stay together in a lawsuit. The 
absence of instructions has frustrated that desire. The Court 
has ruled that the filing of an “Exclusion Request Form” by a 


Veteran is insufficient to opt his or her spouse or children out 
of the class action. 


d. Publication Notice Did Not Cure The 

Defects in Written Noti 

The defects in sending or in failing to send written notice 
cannot be cured. The Supreme Court has declared that 
subdivision (c)(2).expresses an “unambiguous requirement” 
that “individual notice must be provided to those class 
members who are identifiable through reasonable effort.” 
Eisen, 417 U.S. at 175-76. Publication notice may not be 
used as a substitute when class members are identifiable 
through a reasonable effort. When used to provide notice to 
chose who cannot be identified through a reasonable effort, 
publication notice must be the “best practical under all the 
circumstances” and “such as one [198] desirous of actually 
informing the absentee might reasonably adopt to accomplish 
it.” Mullane, 339 U.S. at 315. 

Requesting proprietary radio and television stations to 
broadcast the notice as a public service is not the “best 
practical notice under the circumstances” and clearly is not 
“such as one desirous of actually informing an absentee 
might reasonably adopt to accomplish it.” According to Ms. 
Jan Levin, only 70 radio stations in the whole country and 
not a single television station advised the PMC, in 


180 


accordance with the Court’s request, that the station had 
broadcast the notice. See, Exhibit 1, see also, 100 F.R.D. at 
730. Clearly, the maxim that one gets what one pays for is 
applicable here. One desirous of actually informing those 
who could not be identified through a reasonable effort 
would have paid for air time. That clearly would have been 
the best practical notice under the circumstances. 


Nor is the publication of an abbreviated notice in the 
letters section of the April 30, 1984 edition of Time 
Magazine the best notice under the circumstances and such 
as a person desirous of actually informing an absentee might 
reasonably adopt. A copy of the Time notice is found as 
Exhibit 17. A class member could have obtained a copy of 
the April 30, 1984 Time magazine from the stands as early as 
April 33, 1984. More than likely, he or she would have 
received the magazine by mail at home shortly before or after 
May 1, 1984. The deadline for opting out of the class action 
was May 1, 1984. The notice was abbreviated and required a 
class member to call an 800 number to obtain through the 
mail a complete notice. As can be seen from [199] the April 
4, 1984 letter of Keith D. Snyder, the 800 service had its bad 
days. See, Exhibit 18. If an absentee had seen the Time 
Magazine notice on April 23, 1984, the earliest day on which 
it could have been obtained, and immediately called the 800 
number, he would have gotten the written notice on May 23, 
1984, advising him of his right to opt out by May /, 1984. 
Perhaps most egregious was placing the notice in the letters 
to the editor section instead of on the sports page; perhaps 
counsel for those who wished to opt-out would not have then 
missed the notice. 

The Court required notice to be published in only two 
major newspapers: The New York Times and U.S.A. Today. 
In Re Agent Orange Product Liability Litigation, 100 F.R.D. 
at 730. Publication of an abbreviated notice in The New 
York Times and in U.S.A Today is also not the best notice 
under the circumstances and clearly not such notice as a 
person desirous of informing an absentee in Peoria, Illinois 


181 


would choose. According to official 1 . 
Peoria Illinois has 365,864 people ~ ‘ae 
metropolitan Statistical area.” See, Census of Population 
1980: Illinois; “Characteristics of Inhabitants: Illinois” Table 
11, p. 15-50 and “General Population Character, Illinois,” p. 
q 5-5. As can be seen from the affidavit found as Exhibit 19 
‘241 Sunday editions of The New York ‘Times are regularly 
sold in the Peoria, Illinois metropolitan area and 127 daily 
editions of The New York Times are regularly sold in the 
Peoria, Illinois metropolitan area.” For the purposes of 
providing notice, U.S.A. Today’s circulation is little better. 
According to the affidavit found as Exhibit [200] 20, U.S.A 
Today sells on the average 1450 papers a day. "Peoria, 
Illinois IS a Major metropolitan area. How many New York 
Times and U.S.A. Todays are sold in Mount Stonmn West 
Virginia? 
As the hearing on February 6, 1985, Thomas Henderson 
a member of the PMC, admitted that class certification notice 
had been inadequate. Hearing before the Honorable Jack B. 
Weinstein, February 6, 1985, Tr., pp. 44-45. More 
importantly, he admitted that the PMC’s failure to give 
adequate notice was one of the reasons why the case was 
settled for an inadequate sum. /d. Those admissions raise 
serious questions as to the propriety of the settlement. 
Query, how can class action counsel, who have a fiduciary 
duty to class members, use their malfeasance as a ground for 
accepting an inadequate settlement? If the PMC knew that it 
was incapable of providing constitutional notice, the PMC 
had a fiduciary responsibility to the class members to request 
that the class action be decertified. A failure to give adequate 
notice could not be allowed to adversely affect settlement 
negotiations, which, according to Mr. Henderson, it clearly 
did. If the Court knew that class certification notice had been 
inadequate, it had an absolute duty to require adequate notice 
or decertify the class action. Eisen. 
The class action representatives clearly failed to give 
best practical notice under the deieniennen eodine 


182 


written notice to those class members who could have been 
identified through a reasonable effort. Having failed to give 
constitutionally mandated notice, the class representatives 
used [201] that failure to justify the acceptance of a clearly 
inadequate, unreasonable and unfair settlement. The Court 
must decertify the class action and reject the tainted 
settlement. 
4. The Court Had No Jurisdiction 
Over The Class Action 
The class action was based on diversity of citizenship 
pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1332. In Re Agent Orange Product 
Liability Litigation, 635 F.2d 987, 993, cert. denied, 454 U.S. 
1128 (1981); cf, In re Diamond Shamrock Chemicals Co., 
725 F.2d 858, 861 n.2 (2nd Cir. . 1984). In a class action 
based on diversity of citizenship, each member of the class 
must have a colorable claim of $10,000.00 exclusive of costs 


and interest. Zahn v. International Paper Co., 414 U.S. 291 _ 


(1973). Those class members who have not manifested 
injury clearly have no colorable claim of at least $10,000.00 
in controversy exclusive of costs and interest. The Court is 
without subject matter jurisdiction. 

Under Snyder v. Harris, 394 U.S. 332 (1968) and 
Supreme Tribe of Ben-Hur v. Cauble, 255 U.S. 356 (1921), 
complete diversity must exist between the class 
representatives and the defendants. The Dow Chemical 
Company has it principal place of business and is 
incorporated in Midland, Michigan. Danny and Christine 
Ford, two of the class action representatives were residents of 
Michigan when the action was filed. In the absence of 
complete diversity, the Court is without subject matter 
jurisdiction.

a few clients being represented by this law firm, it is clear

One of the factors that gave the Court pause, when 
Ashcraft and Gerel asked for attorneys fees in connection 
with the class action, was based on this conflict where 
Ashcraft and Gerel took the position that they were not going 
to support the settlement, they were going to advise the 
clients to oppose the settlement, and yet they wanted a

The Court gave a fee, although it has great reservations 
about it, and that matter should be taken up by the Second 
Circuit. 

The practical problem here is that were Ashcraft and 
Gerel disqualified, it is unlikely that any responsible lawyer 
would undertake the representation [8] of these plaintiffs. 
Ashcraft and Gerel must make a very substantial investment 
in the case, both for the appeals that are pending, in 
connection with the motions and other litigation that is going 
forward. In effect, they have gotten on a tiger that they 
cannot dismount. It is unlikely that any other reputable 
attorney would get on that tiger, given the circumstances of 
this case. So that if the motions were granted, the net effect 
would be that these plaintiffs would be unrepresented. It 
seems to me that the second circuit’s position with respect to 
disqualification of attorneys is highly unrealistic in a case 
like this. Therefore, because of the special circumstances, I 
am not going to follow the second circuit’s general rules on 
conflict, even though I recognize that there is a substantial 
conflict here. Motion denied. The second matter on the 
agenda, appeal of defendants from the magistrate’s order 
directing depositions of Doctors Bardana, Hartung, and

When this case was brought, there were only 16,000 
plaintiffs who filed in the cases. Many of those were not 
vets, they were wives and children. 16,000 had come 
forward and filed cases against the chemical companies. 
Those who filed had local attorneys. They were prepared for 
trial before local juries. I think there would have been 
reasonable results, regardless of whether a win or loss, the 
case would have been adjudicated, and there would have 
been settlements which the clients would have been familiar 
and which the clients would have been happy with. Rather 
the case was certified as class action and an imprecise 
definition was fashioned. The class was to include all those 
veterans who were claiming injury, a claim of injury is a 
subjective state, impossible to determine who was in fact a 
class member. Having set up an imprecise definition, there 
was a problem providing notice to all those that might be 
class members. Having had a chance to review the record on 
notice, it is my conclusion that notice was horribly 
inadequate. There were 2.5 million Viet Nam veterans, and 
an additional number of wives, an additional number of 
children.

REPLY BRIEF OF APPELLANTS 
(Objectors to the Class Action Settlement) 
(Reply to Chemical Companies’ Brief_ 


[Excerpts] 


* * * * 


[15] The Chemical Companies note that many individual 
suits filed elsewhere were multidistricted to the Eastern 
District of New York for pretrial purposes. The pertinence of 
that assertion is unclear, for the multidistricted cases were all 
dismissed and it is a separate, class action suit that is here on 
appeal. Are the Chemical Companies arguing that, whenever 
one is multidistricted to a distant state for pretrial purposes 
under 28 U.S.C. 1407, he is then deemed to be there for all 
purposes and may be subject to personal jurisdiction in any 


196 


matter whatsoever? The Chemical Companies cite no 
authority for that proposition; nor can they, for the 
proposition is so violative of traditional notions of personal 
jurisdiction to be devoid of precedent as well as of reason. 

The Erie Railroad-Arrowsmith line of cases and the 
usurpation-of-legislative-power proposition put forth in the 
Appellants’ Main Brief are completely ignored. We suggest 
that the reason is clear: neither the PMC nor the Chemical 
Companies were successful in finding any authority or any 
line of reasoning to the contrary, based upon our legal 
system, the separation of powers between judiciary and 
legislature, and the balance between federal and state 
authority in diversity cases long settled by Erie Railroad and 
by Arrowsmith. On these grounds alone, reversal is required. 

F. Notice. The Chemical Companies’ argument on 
notice is most perplexing. Clearly, no one knew how many 
persons who served [16] in Vietnam had been exposed to 
Agent Orange, had manifested injury from Agent Orange or 
would manifest such injury in the future, or who they were. 
All that was known as [sic] that they were within the 2.4 
million persons who served in Vietnam. A notice sent to 
each of those persons who could be found, plus extensive 
publication, would insure that notice reached al persons 
possible who were included in the pay The ro ee 
Companies object to sending notice to is “overi 
group” (Br. 37). But they do not complain about sending 
notice to an underinclusive group, as admittedly was done 
here. We respectfully suggest that this attitude—which 
permits the wiping out of thousands of personal, individual 
claims without notice—because of the desire not to furnish 
notice to an “overinclusive group” of all those who were ina 
position to be exposed to Agent Orange is indicative of the 


” The Chemical Companies of course do not even attempt to explain 
the pertinence of the multidistricted cases to those persons swept into the 
class who had not been a party to a multidistricted case. 


— 


197 


callous attitude of the Appellees toward the members of the 
class involved in this case. 


The Chemical Companies tell this Court (Br. 38) that 
available lists (the VADS files and lists of Navy and Marine 
service persons) did not include the names and addresses of 
veterans discharged before 1973 and Air Force Reserve 
records go back only into the early of mid 70s: But 
Chemical Companies furnish no reason not to use the lists for 
those who were on it. Apparently, here underinclusiveness, 
which the Chemical Companies seem to champion 
elsewhere, is wrong. The Chemical Companies tell us that 
money spent searching government files would produce only 
an underinclusive list. Somehow, we respectfully submit, the 
logic that it is better to give no notice to those persons than it 
is to give notice to [17] those who could be found appears to 
be lacking: certainly it is not supplied by the Chemical 
Companies. 

The issue appears to come down to whether it is 
reasonable to spend money to find the names and addresses 
of all possible class members before their individual, 
personal causes of action are wiped out. The Chemical 
Companies would say no. To them it is more reasonable to 
wipe out individual, personal causes of action without notice 
than to spend the funds necessary to find the members of the 
class that, by the Chemical Companies’ own admission (Br. 
38) are findable from available lists. That, we respectfully 
Suggest, is not due process, and is not sustainable under 
Mullane v. Hanover Bank, 339 U.S. 306 (1950), or Eisen v. 
Carlisle, 417 U.S. 156 (1974). Moreover, it runs directly 

contrary to the Supreme Court's most recent holding in 
Phillips Petroleum v. Shutts, 105 S.Ct. 2965 (1985), where 
only those persons actually notified were included in the 
class. 

Counsel for the Chemical Companies do tell us, 
however, that "by 1983, virtually all Vietnam veterans who 
were concerned about possible exposure to Agent Orange 


198 


undoubtedly were included by the Veterans Administration 
on the Agent Orange Registry or were aware of the class 
action." Br. 35. We respectfully submit that this unsworn 
testimony by counsel is not worthy of a case of this 
importance, of this Court, or the stature of counsel. 
Moreover, it ignores the record. One need only look at the 
affidavits filed by the Eddelmon group of veteran to see that 
many veterans did not

PLAINTIFFS 
1. The Plaintiffs are Joe Isaacson and his wife Phyllis 


Lisa Isaacson, who reside at 308 Ashford Road, Toms River, 
New Jersey. Joe Isaacson (“Plaintiff’) served in Vietnam 
during the hostilities from 1968 to 1969 where he served as a 
crew chief for the 615 attack fighter squadron stationed at 
Phang Rang. While he was in Vietnam, he was exposed to 
the Defendants’ product, Agent Orange herbicides with 
dioxin contamination, and sustained various injuries alleged 
herein.

ACTS OF DEFENDANTS 
12. During the period of 1961 to 1972 each of the 
corporate Defendants designed, manufactured, marketed 
distributed and sold to the United States Government the 
phenoxy herbicides (hereinafter collectively described as 
Agent Orange herbicides), known as Agent Orange, Orange 


Il, Purple, Pink and Green, and whose basic component 
formulations were: 


a. Agent Orange was a 50/50 mixture of the n- 
butyl esters of 2,4-D and 2,4,5-T (2,4,5- 
tricholorophenox yacetic acid); 

b. Agent Orange II was a 50/50 mixture of the 
isooctyl ester of 2,4,5-T and the n-butyl ester of 
2,4-D; 

c. Agent Purple was a 50/30/20 by weight mixture 
of the n-butyl ester of 2,4,-D, n-butyl ester of 
2,4,5-T and isobutyl ester of 2,4,5-T; 


d. Agent Pink was a 60/40 by weight mixture of n- 


butyl ester of 2,4,5-T and the isobutyl ester of 
2,4,5-T; 


e. Agent Green was a single component 
formulation consisting of n-butyl of 2,4,5-T. 


202 


13. The Agent Orange herbicides manufactured and 
sold by the Defendants to the United States Government for 
use in Vietnam during the aforesaid period contained deadly 
toxic contaminants including dioxins which were inherently 
dangerous to human health and capable of causing severe 
injuries to persons exposed thereto, one of which dioxins is 
the most toxic and deadly, that being 2,3,7,8- 
tetrachlorodibenzo para dioxin, more commonly referred to 
as “TCDD” or “dioxin”. 

14. Each Defendant’s Agent Orange herbicides, as so 
supplied to the military for use in Vietnam contained the 
toxic contaminant dioxin, was defective, and unreasonably 


dangerous to the plaintiff.

CLAIMS UNDER FRAUD AND 
MISREPRESENTATION 

73. The Plaintiff adopts, repeats, reiterates and realleges 
each and every allegation contained in the above Paragraphs. 

74. Each Defendant knew its product was hazardous to 
human health and contaminated. Each Defendant knew that 
the other Defendants’ herbicides were hazardous and 
contaminated with dioxin. None of the Defendants warned 


212 


the uninformed, less expert, military, or informed it either of 
the toxicity of the herbicides or of the dioxin contamination 
and the means of its avoidance or elimination. 


75. The Defendants instead falsely and fraudulently 
represented to the United States Government Officials, 
conspired and acted in concert secretly and persistently to 
deceive the military and other governmental agencies about 
the matters hereinbefore alleged through misrepresentations, 


suppression and withholding of information and other 


schemes. 


76. The Defendants knew that each other’s herbicides 
were dangerous to human health and contaminated with 
dioxin; that each other’s products constituted a breach of 
duty to warn, and that each was negligent in the design and 
production of the Agent Orange herbicides being supplied to 
the government or use in Vietnam and that exposure to such 
herbicides could and would cause injuries to the Plaintiff. 


77. The Defendants concurrently, with their conspiracy 
of concealment and failure to warn the military as 
hereinbefore alleged, misrepresented the safety of their 
herbicides to the military and other Government agencies. 
Despite its extensive knowledge of health hazards, a 
Defendants, Dow Chemical Company, misrepresented to the 
United States military that they had been manufacturing 2,4- 
D and 2,4,5-T for over ten years and to the best of their 
knowledge, none of the workmen in their factories have 
shown any ill effects as a result of working with these 
chemicals. 


78. During the years 1966 and 1967, at times when all 
Defendants were selling dioxin contaminated herbicides to 
the military, they formed a Joint Industry Task Force, under 
the auspices of the National Agricultural Chemicals 
Association, an organization owned, operated, managed and 
controlled by the chemical industry whose purpose was to 

-declare Defendant’s Agent orange herbicides to be 
toxicologically safe. During the period of its existence, the 


213 


Defendants’ Task Force misrepresented to and deceived the - 
Government concerning the nature and the extent of the 
toxicological data as to hazards which was locked within the 
Defendants’ confidential files. 


79. The Defendants and each of them knew of health 
hazards associated with exposure to such herbicides and each 
of them during the period in question either made affirmative 
Statements misrepresenting material facts concerning such 
hazards or willfully did not disclose such material facts by 
conscious and willful omission. 


80. By reason of the Defendants’ such 
misrepresentations, the military and the Government were 
lulled into a belief that the Defendants’ Agent Orange 
herbicides were safe to human health, and the military was 
prevented from taking the actions described in the above 
Paragraphs. 

81. The Agent Orange herbicides manufactured by the 
Defendants were not produced in accordance with military 
specifications set forth in their Government contracts. 


82. The Government contracts, pursuant to which the 
Defendants manufactured their Agent Orange herbicides, did 
not order, request, or put limitations on any permissible level 
of the toxic contaminant dioxin. The herbicides produced by 
the Defendants were generally similar except that they 
contained varying degrees of the toxic contaminant dioxin. 
The Defendants jointly controlled all of the manufacturing 
processes used in the production of the Agent Orange 
herbicides supplied to the military. 

83. Each of the Defendants’ products was defective and 
dangerous to human health, especially in view of the 
presence of the toxic contaminant dioxin. 

84. By reason of the foregoing, each Defendant is 
individually, jointly and severally liable for the injuries 
suffered by the Plaintiff and is responsible for and must pay 
all special, general, compensatory and punitive damages.

PLAINTIFFS 

1. The Plaintiffs are Joe Isaacson and his wife Phyllis 
Lisa Isaacson, who reside at 308 Ashford Road, Toms River, 
New Jersey. Joe Isaacson (‘Plaintiff’) served in Vietnam 
during the hostilities from 1968 to 1969 where he served as a 
crew chief for the 615 attack fighter squadron stationed at 
Phang Rang. While he was in Vietnam, he was exposed to 
the Defendants’ product, Agent Orange herbicides with 
dioxin contamination, and sustained various injuries alleged 
herein. 


DEFENDANTS 

2. Defendant Dow Chemical Company is a Delaware 
corporation with its principal place of business in Midland, 
Michigan.

corporation with its principal place of business in St. Louis 
Missouri. 

4. Defendant American Home Products, Inc. is a

New York corporation with its principal place of business in 
Dallas, Texas.

ACTS OF DEFENDANTS 


12. During the period of 1961 to 1972 each of the 
corporate Defendants designed, manufactured, marketed, 
distributed and sold to the United States Government the 
phenoxy herbicides (hereinafter collectively described as 
Agent Orange herbicides), known as Agent Orange, Orange 
fl, Purple, Pink and Green, and whose basic component 
formulations were: 


a. Agent Orange was a 50/50 mixture of the n- 
butyl esters of 2,4-D and 2,4,5-T (2,4,5- 
tricholorophenox yacetic acid); 

b. Agent Orange II was a 50/50 mixture of the 


og ester of 2,4,5-T and the n-butyl ester of 


c. Agent Purple was a 50/30/20 by weight mixture 
of the n-butyl ester of 2,4,-D, n-butyl ester of 
2,4,5-T and isobutyl ester of 2,4,5-T; 


220 


d. Agent Pink was a 60/40 by weight mixture of n- 
butyl ester of 2,4,5-T and the isobutyl ester of 
2,4,5-T; 

e. Agent Green was a_ single component 
formulation consisting of n-butyl of 2,4,5-T. 


13. The Agent Orange herbicides manufactured and 
sold by the Defendants to the United States Government for 
use in Vietnam during the aforesaid period contained deadly 
toxic contaminants including dioxins which were inherently 
dangerous to human health and capable of causing severe 
injuries to persons exposed thereto, one of which dioxins is 
the most toxic and deadly, that being 2,3,7,8- 
tetrachlorodibenzo para dioxin, more commonly referred to 
as “TCDD” or “dioxin”. 

14. Each Defendant’s Agent Orange herbicides, as so 
supplied to the military for use in Vietnam contained the 
toxic contaminant dioxin, was defective, and unreasonably 
dangerous to the plaintiff. 


INJURY TO PLAINTIFF 

15. As direct and proximate results of the acts described 
above, the Plaintiff was caused to suffer grievous, serious, 
and severe injuries, and sustained serious and permanent 
injuries to his health, strength and activity, and severe shock 
to his nervous system, including but not limited to non- 
Hodgkin’s lymphoma, which was manifested and diagnosed 
less than two years before commencement of this action and 
which perforated his colon and involved the small bowel, 
requiring a resection of the right colon and terminal ileum, 
constant diarrhea and was caused to suffer and will continue 
to suffer physical injuries and mental pain, a loss of earnings 
and impairment of future earning capacity. 

16. As a further proximate result of the defendants’ 
wrongful conduct, plaintiff was required to, and did, employ 
physicians and surgeons to examine, treat and care for him, 
and plaintiff incurred, and will incur, hospital, medical and 
other and additional incidental expenses. 


221 


17. As a further proximate result of the defendants’ 
wrongful conduct, wife plaintiff was deprived of her 
husband’s services, companionship and consortium.

Deadline for Application to Agent Orange Payment 
Program Extended 


Brooklyn, New York, December 29, 1994 - 


United States District Court Judge Jack B. Weinstein 
today ordered an extension of the deadline for Vietnam 
Veterans to file applications to the Agent Orange Veteran 
Payment Program, the program established to distribute a 
portion of the proceeds of the Agent Orange Settlement 
Fund. The Payment Program will now accept all 
applications submitted with a postmark date of January 17, 
1995. Applications must be submitted to P.O. Box 110, 
Hartford, CT, 06104. To obtain an application, veterans 
should contact the Payment Program at 1-800-225-4712. 
The Claims Administrator has expanded the number of 
telephone lines and operators to accommodate incoming calls 
and has extended the hours of operation through the end of 
December from 8 am. until 12 midnight. 


The Payment Program was established by Court order in 
1985, and commenced operations in 198[illegible] after the 
conclusion of appeals. The Program had originally been 
scheduled to conclude its operations as of December 31, 
1994. That deadline had been widely publicized both 
through direct communications to the class and various 
informational materials distributed to the print and radio 
media, veterans groups and various public agencies, both 
throughout the operation of the Program and more recently 
during 1994. Nevertheless, the Court has concluded that due 
to the fact that a number of applications were submitted 
during the last weeks of the Program and due to the fact that 
a number of inquiries were received by the Payment Program 
in the last week of 1994, the deadline should be extended to 


235 


permit those who have only recently decided to ly for 
benefits to submit their application materials. — 


The Agent Orange Payment Program is a_ benefits 
program established in 1985 solely to distribute the proceeds 
of the Agent Orange Settlement Fund. It has no relationship 
to programs operated by the Department of Veterans’ Affairs 
Or programs operated by other governmental agencies. 
Pursuant to statute and regulation, veterans may continue to 
apply to the Department of Veterans Affairs for various 
benefits offered to certain Vietnam veterans claiming 
exposure to Agent Orange. Veterans interested in such 
programs should contact their local Department of Veterans 
Affairs office. The National Veterans Legal Services 
Program, a Program established by the Agent Orange 
Settlement Fund, has published a “Self-Help Guide on Agent 
Orange”. Vietnam veterans and their families may obtain 
that guide free of charge by writing to NVLSP, 2001 S 
Street, N.W., Suite 610, Washington, D.C. 20009. 


The Agent Orange Class Assistance Program, the 
Program established to distribute another portion of the 
Agent Orange Settlement Fund for the benefit of the families 
and children of Vietnam veterans will continue operations 
through the middle of 1996. 


236 


UNITED STATE DISTRICT COURT 
EASTERN DISTRICT OF NEW YORK 


In re Agent Orange 
Product Liability Litigation 


MDL 381 
[Filed: Dec. 22, 1994] 


Order 


In accordance with previous Orders of this Court, the 
Agent Orange Veteran Payment Program is scheduled to 
accept for processing all applications submitted to that 
Program postmarked December 31, 1994, and to deny as 
untimely any applications postmarked after that date. This 
deadline was publicized in all communications to class 
members and in releases issued to the media and to veterans 
organizations and other public service agencies since the 
order establishing the Program in 1985. The class was 
further notified of this deadline in direct communications to 
individual applicants to the Program and through the media 
and veterans organizations during 1994. Nevertheless, 
during the last week of 1994, the Claims Administrator of the 
Payment Program has received substantial requests for 
application materials from class members who had not 
previously sought to contact the Payment Program. The 
Payment Program has also received a substantially greater 
volume of inquiries than expected and although the Claims 
Administrator has increased its staff and the number of 
incoming telephone lines, the Claims Administrator has 
advised that a number of individual callers have been unable 
to reach the Program as the year end approaches in order to 
request an application kit. 

In light of these circumstances, the Court has concluded 
that the deadline for submission of applications forms should 
be extended for a modest period of time to permit those who 


237 


have only recently decided to apply to the Program to submit 
their application materials. It therefore Ordered that the 
deadline for submission of applications to the Agent Orange 
Veteran Payment Program shall be extended so that the 
Program shall accept for processing all applications 
— with a postmark dated on or before January 17, 

So Ordered.
