FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE 


United States District Court Announces Plan for 
Terminating the Operations of the Agent Orange 
Settlement Fund 

August 10, 1994, Brooklyn, N.Y.—United States 
District Court Judge Jack B. Weinstein today announced the 
plan for concluding the distribution activities of the Agent 
Orange Settlement Fund. Based on that plan, the Agent 
Orange Settlement Fund will accept applications for payment 
from individual veterans and survivors of deceased veterans 
through December 31, 1994. The Settlement Fund wilJ 
continue to fund grant programs of agencies providing 
specified services for Vietnam veterans until June, 1996. 

The Agent Orange Settlement Fund was created by the 
settlement in May of 1984 of the Agent Orange Product 
Liability Litigation, a class action lawsuit brought by 
Vietnam veterans and their families against the chemical 
companies that manufactured certain herbicides used by the 
United States Armed Forces during the Vietnam War. The 
action was settled for the aggregate sum of $180 million. 
Distribution of the Settlement Fund assets began in 1989, 
after the conclusion of all appeals to the settlement. 


The Court established two programs to distribute the 
Fund to and for the benefit of Vietnam veterans in the United 
States. The Agent Orange Veteran Payment Program was 
established to provide cash compensation to totally disabled 
veterans and to survivors of deceased veterans. Since its 
inception, the Payment Program has _ distributed 
approximately $163 million (including principal and 
earnings) to over 37,500 veterans and survivors. At the time 
the Payment Program concludes, it is expected to have 


OR OM AO, 


241 


distributed approximately $181 million to over 42,000 class 
members. 


The Agent Orange Class Assistance Program was 
established to provide grant awards to organizations 
providing services for the benefit of Vietnam veterans and 
their families. The goal of the Class Assistance Program was 
to provide monies to fulfill service needs of the Vietnam 
veteran community not being met by existing service 
networks. The emphasis of the Class Assistance Program is 
on providing monies for programs of benefit to the children 
of Vietnam veterans and on creating a national network of 
assistance and informational programs. Since it began its 
grant-making activities, the Class Assistance Program has 
distributed grants totalling $57.7 million to 84 different 
agencies operating in all 50 states plus Washington, D.C. and 
Puerto Rico. The various service programs supported by 
these grants have provided direct benefits for an estimated 
180,000 veterans and families of veterans. By the time it 
concludes, the Class Assistance Program will have 
distributed a total of $70 million in grants. 


The monies allocated to the Payment Program and the 
Assistance Program will be distributed for the benefit of the 
plaintiff class members. No monies will be returned to the 
chemical companies or retained by the Court for any purpose. 
Accordingly, by the end of 1996, all settlement monies, 
including all earnings of the Settlement Fund should be fully 
disbursed. It is anticipated that by the time of its conclusion, 
the Settlement Fund will have distributed, in total, 
approximately $269.8 million consisting of $181 million in 
cash payments through the Payment Program, $70 million in 
grant awards through the Class Assistance program, $5 
million to the trusts established in Australia and New 
Zealand and $13.8 million in fees to plaintiffs’ attorneys. By 
the time the Fund is fully disbursed, administrative costs 
incurred in distributing and managing the Fund’s assets are 
expected to equal approximately 9 percent of the amount 
distributed. 


242 


The Agent Orange Veteran Payment Program will accept 
for processing all completed applications submitted with a 
postmark of December 31, 1994. All applications submitted 
timely will be reviewed and considered for payment. To 
obtain an application or information about the Program, 
veterans may call toll free 1-800-225-4712 (from 8 a.m. to 6 
p.m. Eastern Time). Class members may also write to the 
Payment Program at P.O. Box 110, Hartford, CT 06104. 
The Payment Program is expected to complete the processing 
of all applications received by the deadline no later than June 
1995. The Payment Program may have a need to contact 
individuals who have in the past received payment from the 
Payment Program. Any such individuals should contact the 
Payment Program if they have moved. 


The plan for terminating the distribution of the 
Settlement Fund calls for the creation of a permanent archive 
of the records of the Payment Program and the Class 
Assistance Program. It is the recommendation of the Court- 
appointed Board of Advisors of both Programs (consisting of 
Vietnam veterans) that the information collected during the 
operation of these programs should be preserved and stored 
in a manner that will permit access to both class members 
and the public, as appropriate. The information collected 
provides a wealth of knowledge about the Vietnam veteran 
population that may prove beneficial to the Vietnam veteran 
community.

Please take notice that on the 13th day of December, 
1999 at 10:00 a.m. in the motion part of this Court to be held 
in the United States Courthouse for the Eastern District of 
New York, Defendants The Dow Chemical Company, 
Monsanto Company, Hercules Incorporated, Occidental 
Chemical Corp., Ultramar Diamond Shamrock Corp., Maxus 
Energy Corp., Chemical Land Holdings, Inc., T-H 
Agriculture & Nutrition Co., Inc., Thompson Hayward 
Chemical Co., Harcros Chemicals, Inc., and Uniroyal, Inc., 
C.D.U. Holding, Inc., Uniroyal Chemical Company will 
move this Court, pursuant to Rule 12(b)(6) of the Federal 
Rules of Civil Procedure for an Order dismissing this action 
against the defendants with prejudice.

accompanying Affidavit of Steven Brock, dated October 12, 
1999 and exhibits attached thereto, and the accompanying 
Memorandum of Law. No prior request for the foregoing 
relief has been made. Accordingly, defendants’ motion for 
dismissal with prejudice should be granted.

NATURE OF CASE

an unreasonably dangerous product and placed it into the 
stream of commerce. Through the joint and combined

tetrachlorodibenzo-p-dioxin (e.g. TCDD or dioxin), thought 
to be one of the most toxic substances known to man.

controversy exceeds $75,000.00.

ORDERED REGARDING SUBMISSION AND 
ACCEPTANCE OF THE FINAL REPORT OF THE 
SPECIAL MASTER ON THE DISTRIBUTION OF THE 
AGENT ORANGE SETTLEMENT FUND 

The Special Master has submitted to this Court the Final 
Report of the Special Master on the Distribution of the Agent 
Orange Settlement Fund, dated September 24, 1997. In the 


261 


Report, the Special Master advises that the distribution 
activities of the Agent Orange Settlement Fund have 
concluded and that all Settlement Fund assets have been 
expended and committed through the programs established 
by this Court for distribution of the Fund. The Report notes 
that certain minor administrative activities necessary to 
conclude finally the activities of the Fund are ongoing but 
that such activities will not affect in any way the prior 
disposition of Settlement Fund assets in accordance with the 
plan for distribution of the Fund. The Report documents the 
manner in which the Settlement Fund assets were distributed 
and expended throughout the duration of the Settlement Fund 
from May 1954 (the date of the settlement) through the 
present and includes an accounting of the revenues and 
expenses of the Fund. Thus, the Report constitutes the final 
report on the disposition of the fund assets subject to the 
minor activities and functions rémaining to be completed as 
described in the Report. 

The Report of the Special Master is accepted and 
approved for filing and docketing and is confirmed as the 


Final Report on the Distribution of the Agent Orange 
Settlement Fund. 

The Clerk of the Court is directed to file and docket this 
Report and to forward a copy of the Report to the Clerk of 
the Court for the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Second Circuit for transmission to the Chief Judge of that 
Court. 


SO ORDERED.

PART I : OVERVIEW: SETTLEMENT, 
DISTRIBUTION PLAN AND IMPLEMENTATION 
STRUCTURE 


The Agent Orange Settlement Fund was created by the 
resolution of a class action litigation commenced in 1979 by 
and on behalf of Vietnam veterans and their family members. 
The class complaint alleged that the veterans had been 
injured by exposure to herbicides used by United States 
Armed Forces as defoliants during the Vietnam War. (For 
purposes of simplicity, the various herbicides used are 
referred to collectively by the term “Agent Orange” — the 
name by which one of those herbicides was identified during 
the war.) The defendants in the action consisted of various 
chemical companies that had manufactured the herbicides for 
use by the Armed Forces of the United States government. 
At the time of its commencement, the Agent Orange 
litigation was reported to be the largest mass tort litigation 
ever filed. 

The District Court initially certified the action as a class 
proceeding in 1980. In re “Agent Orange” Product Liability 
Litigation, 506 F. Supp. 762 (E.D.N.Y. 1980), modified, 100 
F.R.D. 718 (E.D.N.Y. 1983), mandamus denied, 725 F.2d 
858 (2d Cir.). cert. denied, 465 U.S. 1067 (1984), aff'd, 818 
F.2d 145 (2d Cir. 1987), cert. denied, 484 U.S. 1004 (1988). 
The class as certified was defined as “those persons who 
were in the United States, New Zealand or Australian Armed 
Forces at any time from 1961 to 1972 who were injured 
while in or near Vietnam by exposure to Agent Orange or 
other phenoxy herbicides, including those composed in 

whole or in part of 2, 4, 5-trichlorophenoxyacetic acid or 
containing some amount of 2, 3, 7, 8-tetrachlorodibenzo-p- 
dioxin. The class also includes spouses, parents, and 
children of the veterans born before January 1, 1984 directly 
or derivatively injured as a result of the exposure.” 100 
F.R.D. at 729. The Court estimated that the class included 


—_— - 


263 


between 2 and 3 million veterans and 8 to 10 million family 
members. 


Notice of the class was distributed during March 19 
(after the Second Circuit vacated the stay it had Sead 
conjunction with a petition for mandamus that had been filed 
challenging the class). The class members were given the 
Opportunity to withdraw from the class, and by the end of the 
opt-out period, 2,440 class members had in fact elected to 
withdraw from the class. (A separate class was certified as a 
mandatory non-opt-out class on the issue of punitive 
damages, and class members were prohibited from 
withdrawing from that class.) 


. The Court scheduled trial to commence in May of 1984 
using initially ten plaintiffs asserting claims characteristic of 
the allegations of the class. On May 7, 1984 — the day jury 
selection was to commence — the parties agreed to settle the 
class litigation for the aggregate sum of $180 million. The 
defendants paid the aggregate settlement amount including 
accrued interest into the Court-established account between 
May 1984 and January 1985. 


The settlement agreement did not provide an i 
guidelines as to the distribution of the A anand Fund to re 
plaintiff class. Rather, the agreement set forth only general 
requirements and specified that the Settlement Fund was to 
be maintained and administered by the Court and remain 
under the Court’s continuous jurisdiction, control and 
SUPErVisioa. The Court was granted the authority to retain 
auditors, special masters, disbursing agents, consultants and 
counsel and to enter into contracts. 

The Court held extensive hearings at different locations 
around the country in order to solicit the views of the class 
members as to the fairness of the settlement and the 
appropriate mechanism for distributing the Settlement Fund 
assets. The Court heard the public statements of hundreds of 
witnesses and class members, and it reviewed hundreds of 
letters and written submissions from class members detailing 


264 


their views regarding how the Settlement Fund should be 
used to benefit the class. At the conclusion of the hearings 
and after the review of the comments of the class, the Court 
issued an extensive opinion and order analyzing the 
comments of the class members, evaluating the legal 
positions of the parties an¢ assessing the terms of the 
settlement. Based on that smeiysis, the Court preliminarily 
approved the settlement as tair, reasonable and adequate in 
accordance with appiicable federal rules but reserved a final 
decision on approval of the settlement pending the 
development of a plan for distributing the assets of the 
settlement.’ 


The Court then requested the submission of plans for the 
distribution of the Settlement Fund assets and requested that 
the Special Masters previously appointed — Kenneth R. 
Feinberg and Richard J. Davis — assist in coordinating the 
consideration of distribution options and managing the 
investment of Settlement Fund assets.’ In responding to the 


? The Court, in fact, approved the settlement on January 7, 1985 and 
reaffirmed that decision on June 18, 1985 based on information submitted 
regarding possible mechanisms for distribution. See In re “Agent 
Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 611 F. Supp.1296, 1346 (E.D.N.Y. 1985), 
modified, 818 F.2d 226 (2d Cir. 1987). The Court issued a Final Order 
and Judgment of Dismissal on July 9, 1985. Ryan v. Dow Chem. Co., 
618 F. Supp. 623 (E.D.N.Y. 1985). 


265 


Court's request, the Special Masters obtained the advice and 
assistance of a 29-member group of Vietnam-era veterans. 


The development of a fair mechanism for distributing 
the Settlement Fund assets to the class was a significant 
challenge: first, as discussed in the Court’s Opinion 
reviewing the settlement terms, the plaintiffs lacked adequate 
proof to establish a causal link between the exposure to 
herbicides and the multitude of illness and injuries claimed. 
See In re “Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 597 F. Supp. 
740, 782 (E.D.N.Y. 1984), aff'd, 818 F.2d 145 (2d Cir. 
1987), cert. denied, 484 U.S. 1004 (1988). -As the various 
injuries alleged (with perhaps one exception) appeared in the 
general population and were attributable to various causes or 
were of unknown etiology, it was impossible to determine 
which class members among those who might suffer from 
one or more of a large group of claimed diseases, including 
peripheral and central neuropathy, liver disorders, 
gastrointestinal conditions and all tumors and metabolic 
problems, ought to receive a payment from the Settlement 
Fund based on the presence of a particular disease. Second, 
given the vast size of the class, it seemed unreasonable to 
simply distribute the money on some sort of pro-rata basis, 
such as by providing an equal distribution to each class 
member. Indeed, depending on the rate of claims filings, 
class members might obtain payments of as little as $5 or $10 
using such a distribution system. Third, use of the 
Settlement Fund assets to provide some general benefit to the 
class — for example, medical research — seemed duplicative 
of the undertakings of governmental entities and also seemed 
to ignore the real need of some class members for a concrete 
response to their claims. The task, therefore, was to develop 
a plan for distributing the Settlement Fund assets that would 
target the funds in a meaningful way and, to the extent 
possible, augment the benefit to the class by leveraging the 

Ultimately, after additional formal hearings, the Court 
adopted (with slight revision) a plan of distribution prepared 


266 


by the Court-appointed Special Master taking into account 
the input received from the various veteran advisors. See In 
re “Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 611 F. Supp. 1396 
(E.D.N.Y. 1985), modified, 818 F.2d 179 (2d Cir. 1987), 
cert. denied, 487 U.S. 1234, and modified, 689 F. Supp. 1250 
(1988). The plan presented by the Special Master specified 
two mechanisms for the distribution of Settlement Fund 
assets to the class members residing in the United States. 
First, the plan recommended the distribution of cash 
payments to individual class members, including both 
veterans and families of veterans, based generally on the 
severity of the veteran’s medical condition or death (along 
with other factors). The objective of this cash payment 
program was to provide prompt financial benefits to those 
class members most in need. Second, the plan recommended 
the leveraging of Settlement Fund assets for the benefit of the 
class through the establishment of a  grant-making 
“foundation.” The purpose of the grant-making foundation 
was to provide initial funding for organizations to provide 
services and benefits specifically targeted to the needs of the 
class, including, particularly, the children of the veterans. 
The intent of the grant-making program was twofold: first, 
to provide immediate funding to address the un-met needs of 
the class members, and, second, to educate — through 
example — existing social Service organizations and other 
grant-making entities and foundations as to the particular 
needs of the Vietnam veteran community and the class 
members in particular. The hope was that through education 
and provision of directed funding the “foundation” 
established to distribute Settlement Fund assets would 
become and would create a lasting legacy for the Vietnam 
veteran community by institutionalizing a services support 
network directed at the needs of the population of Vietnam 
veterans and their families. 

Upon approval of the plan of distribution, the Court 
commenced the procedures for establishing the 
administrative systems necessary to implement that plan. 


267 


The Court established a request-for-proposal process for the 
selection of a claims administration facility and for the 
selection of investment managers to advise and assist in the 
proper investment of Settlement Fund assets. The request- 
for-proposal process yielded 16 applications from groups and 
entities interested in providing claims services and 39 
applications from financial institutions and other entities 
interested in providing investment and depository services. 
The request-for-proposal process incorporated detailed 
review by the Court, the Special Masters and the Advisory 
Board of Vietnam veterans. The procedures employed for 
the selection of contractors to provide the requisite 
management services for the Court are described below in 
the sections outlining the operation of the distribution 
programs. 

ior to the completion of implementation procedures, 
appeals as to certain aspects of the Court’s decisions 
regarding the settlement were lodged. As a result, the United 
States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit stayed 
implementation of the distribution plan pending the 
resolution of appeals. Finally, on April 21, 1987, the Court’s 
decisions certifying the class, finding the settlement to be 
fair, reasonable and adequate, and adopting the distribution 
plan (with some modification) were affirmed by the United 
States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit. In re 


* The modification to the distribution plan mandated by the Court of 
Appeals concerned only that portion of the distribution plan providing for 
a “foundation” to establish grant programs for the benefit of the class. 
The distribution plan as originally approved by the District Court 
contemplated an independent foundation that would operate with Court 
guidelines but that ultimately could become a wholly-independent entity 
with fund-raising capability that could exist in perpetuity. The Court of 
Appeals rejected the concept of an independent entity, and while 
endorsing the concept of a grant-making program, it required that any 
distribution of assets be directly controlled by the Court. Jn re “Agent 
Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 818 F.2d 179, 185 (2d Cir. 1987), cert. 
denied, 487 U.S. 1234 (1988) (“We are unwilling for several reasons to 


268 


“Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 818 F.2d 145 (2d Cir. 
1987), cert. denied, 484 U.S. 1004 (1988); In re “Agent 
Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 818 F.2d 179 (2d Cir. 1987), 
cert. denied, 487 U.S. 1234 (1988). 


Subsequently, on July 5, 1988, after the Supreme Court 
declined to review any aspect of the case, the Court issued an 
Opinion and Order specifying the final terms for distribution 
of the Settlement Fund assets. In re “Agent Orange” Prod. 
Liab. Litig., 689 F. Supp. 1250 (E.D.N.Y. 1988) (“Final 
Distribution Opinion”). 

The Final Distribution Opinion adopted by the Court 
established the two mechanisms outlined above for the 
distribution of Settlement Fund assets to and for the benefit 
of the class members in the United States. First, the Court 
established the Agent Orange Veteran Payment Program. 
The function of this program was to distribute cash payments 
to certain individual class members. The concept defining 
the Payment Program was that a distribution plan for the 
settlement of a tort action should reflect traditional tort 
concepts in the sense that individuals should receive 
compensatory payments for their claimed injuries. See In re 
“Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 611 F. Supp. 1396, 1410 
(E.D.N.Y. 1985); In re “Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 
669 F. Supp. 1250 (E.D.N.Y. 1988). The Program's 
guidelines, which are described more fully in Part Il below, 
mandated the distribution of such payment to totally disabled 
Vietnam veterans and to the survivors of deceased Vietnam 
veterans. Because this program was to be the mechanism by 
which veteran class members would receive traditional 
compensatory-type payments, the bulk of the Settlement 
Fund assets were allocated to this program. Second, the court 
This program replaced the original concept of a foundation 


permit the distribution of any settlement proceeds to a largely 
independent foundation.”). 


269 


for the benefit of Vietnam veterans and their families with a 
grant-making program operating for a finite period under 
Court supervision. The mandate of the Class Assistance 
Program was to leverage remaining Settlement Fund assets 
most effectively by providing grants to institutions and 
entities for the purpose of providing services (including 
assistance in accessing other benefits and information) to 
Vietnam veterans and their families. The Class Assistance 
Program is described in detail in Part II] below. In addition, 
the Court allocated Settlement Fund assets to class members 
in New Zealand and Australia based on the percentage of 
Armed Forces in Vietnam from those two countries and 
allocated a portion of the Settlement Fund for attorneys’ fees 
based on an extensive review of attorneys’ fees petitions. 
See In re “Agent Orange” Prod. Liab. Litig., 611 F. Supp. 
1296 (E.D.N.Y. 1985) (Memorandum and Order on 
Attorneys’ Fees As Modified and Final Judgment), aff'd in 
part, rev'd in part, 818 F.2d 226 (2d Cir. 1987); and, 611 F. 
Supp. 1452 (E.D.N.Y. 1985), rev'd, 818 F.2d, cert. denied, 
484 U.S. 926 (1987).

Upon conclusion of the appellate process, the Court 
provided for the immediate implementation of the 
distribution plan in order to provide funds to class members 
as promptly as possible. The Payment Program commenced 
operations in July of 1988 (immediately after the issuance of 
the Court’s Final Distribution Opinion regarding the 
distribution of the Settlement Fund) and began mailing of 
application forms to class members in November of 1988. 
The Class Assistance Program commenced operations in 
January of 1989 and began issuance of initial grants on 
March 30, 1989. A detailed chronology of the 
implementation of the distribution plan with respect to each 
“program” is contained in Parts I and Ill below. 


The programs established by the Court to distribute 
Settlement Fund assets were supervised directly by the Court 
and remained under the Court’s control and jurisdiction at all 
times. The Court appointed various contractors and 
individuals to provide necessary processing, management 
and investment services to accomplish the distribution of the 
Settlement Fund’s assets as described more fully below. The 
Court was assisted throughout the implementation of the 
distribution plan by two Advisory~ Boards of Vietnam 
veterans. The Advisory Board assisting specifically with the 
implementation of the Payment Program consisted of five 
members: Charles T. Hagel, Vincent J. Martin, Jr., John L. 
McElrath, Donna-Marie Boulay, and Albert A. Dandridge,

PART II: THE AGENT ORANG

A. Mandate/Outline of Eligibility Guidelines: 
Distribution Guidelines Established by 
the Court. _ 


The basic outline and guideli istributi 
Settlement Fund assets hon = nde seni ~ 
contained in the Distribution Opinion issued by the Eaten 
May 28, 1985 and the Final Distribution Opinion issued b 
the Court on July 5, 1988. Detailed administration maidens 
and specific claims-processing protocols were developed 
under the Court’s supervision during the implementation of 
the claims-processing facility. The more detailed 
administrative guidelines are described in this Report o 
set forth in various attachments to the Report. sg 

The Payment Program was established to provide the 
— for distributing cash payments to a portion of the 
Class members. The Court-adopted distribution plan was 
eee on the theory that a substantial portion of the 

“ttlement Fund assets should be focused on the veterans 
within the class and, more specifically, on those veterans 
who were most injured or in need of assistance. Concluding 


272 


that there was no realistic basis upon which to distribute the 
Settlement Fund based on the type of disease alleged or the 
nature of the alleged herbicide exposure, the Court approved 
the distribution of Settlement Fund assets based on a finding 
of total disability or death. See In re “Agent Orange” Prod. 
Liab. Litig., 611 F. Supp. 1396, 1400 (E.D.N.Y. 1985) 
(“Because no substantial scientific evidence supports a 
finding of causal connection between Agent Orange exposure 
and any specific disease except chloracne, and because of the 
near impossibility of proving that any particular plaintiff's 
condition was caused by Agent Orange, dividing the fund 
among those with particular diseases is unjustified.”). By 
using the death and disability criteria for the focused 
distribution of a significant portion of the Settlement Fund 
assets, the distribution plan channeled assets in a meaningful 
way to those class members with the most severe conditions. 
(In contrast, any program under which assets of . the 
Settlement Fund would be distributed on a pro-rata basis to 
class members would have resulted in de minimis payments 
to individuals, and any plan that would have distributed 
assets based on some assumed (but unproven) causal 
connection between exposure and certain diseases would 
have distributed potentially significant amounts of money to 
relatively fewer claimants on a purely speculative basis, in 
the Court’s view.) The use of disability and death as the key 
factors for distinguishing among class member claimants 
allowed for ease of administration, an equitable allocation of 
assets to the class as a whole and objective eligibility criteria 
that are both understandable and relatively simple to apply 
fairly among various claimants. 

Under the guidelines established by the Court, to be 
eligible for compensation from the Payment Program the 
class members were required to establish the following: first, 
that the veteran served in or near Vietnam between 1961 and 
1972 as a member of the United States Armed Forces (as 
mandated by the class definition); second, that the class 
member applicant was either a totally-disabled Vietnam 


Oe ~ oes 


273 


veteran or the survivor of a deceased Vietnam veteran who 
served in the United States Armed Forces in Vietnam during 
the requisite period; third, that based on the circumstances of 
the veteran’s service (including location of service and 
particular experiences during service) the veteran met a test 
of probable exposure to herbicides; fourth, that the death or 
disability was not caused by a traumatic or accidental 
occurrence; and, fifth, that the death or disability occurred 
before December 31, 1994 (which was the Court-established 
closure date for the Payment Program). 


The Court-adopted distribution plan provided for an 
eligibility and payout period equivalent to ten years from the 
date of settlement (i.e., from May 1984 when the action was 
settled until December 31, 1994). Under the distribution plan 
any class member who manifested an eligible condition 
during that period and filed an application was permitted to 
obtain a distribution from the Settlement Fund. The ten-year 
period selected reflected a balancing of the interest in prompt 
distribution of Settlement Fund assets to the class and the 
desire to provide benefits for a reasonable length of time after 
the last possible exposure to herbicides. The Program 
closure date in effect was 22 years after the last exposure 
date based on the class definition. 


In order to assure appropriate and equivalent treatment 
of claims, the distribution plan defined the requirements of 
death and disability with reference to standard criteria. 
Veteran class members were eligible for a disability payment 
if they could demonstrate a total, permanent disability as 
defined by the Social Security Act. The veteran need not be 
eligible for or receiving Social Security disability benefits, 
but the claims administration facility was required to review 
the medical documentation to determine the presence of a 
disability consistent with that definition. Any disability was 
eligible except those caused by an accidental, traumatic or 
self-inflicted injury. In other words, disabilities caused by 
various physical and mental ailments except those attributed 
to a specific accidental or traumatic cause (such as, for 


274 


example, a work-related injury) were considered eligible for 
payment. Similarly, survivors of veterans were eligible for 
payment provided the death was not caused by accident, 
trauma or self-infliction. By excluding accidental and 
traumatic deaths and injuries the eligibility criteria eliminated 
deaths and disabilities that could clearly be attributed to a 
source other than herbicide exposure and thereby preserved 
funds for other members of the class. 

Recognizing that requiring class members to 
demonstrate that they had been exposed to herbicides might 
prove quite burdensome, the Court adopted a mechanism to 
determine a probability of exposure to herbicides in order to 
assure compliance with the class definition in distributing 
cash payments to class members. Under the Court’s 
exposure test, claims were reviewed using a computerized 
exposure evaluation system, under which the veteran’s 
location of service in Vietnam was compared to the 
documented locations of herbicide spraying. This objective 
system was implemented through the development of a 
computer database that incorporated extensive information 
about the location of military units and the locations of 
herbicide spray missions and applied certain Court-approved 
parameters for determining a probability of exposure. More 
specifically, the computerized evaluation system matched 
service locations (obtained from the claimant’s exposure 
form and from the documented locations of military units) to 
herbicide spray locations.’ To be considered a match, the 
veteran’s service did not have to occur at the exact time and 
place of spraying. Rather, the system took into account past 
herbicide spraying in an area up to fifteen kilometers from 


7 Detailed information about troop locations and movements was 
obtained from a variety of sources, including military records and data 
correlated from claimants. Troop locations were considered to cover a 
large area in applying the exposure review to account for the fact that 
various members of a unit might be widely dispersed. 


275 


the veteran’s general unit location and assign 
“scores” when the locations matched. ~ caine 
evaluation System added up monthly exposure “scores.” The 
scores” were calculated taking into account both time and 
distance: from herbicide spraying so that a veteran who 
moved into an area in which spraying had previously 
occurred would receive a “score” for that location. Thus, as 
the time between spraying and the veteran’s location in an 
area increased, or as distance increased, the exposure “score” 
decreased. Generally, with the exception of veterans who 
served very late or very early in the war, those who served in 
Marine and Army combat units satisfied the exposure 
eligibility criterion. Those who served in the off-shore Navy 
or at air bases outside the area of herbicide use were most 
commonly found not to be exposed under this system. The 
exposure evaluation system is described in detail in the Final 
Report of the Exposure Evaluation Unit at Attachment B. 


Payment amounts were varied under the guideli 
based on the date of onset of the veteran’s total settee an 
the date of the veteran’s death. Thus, under the payment 
guidelines, those class member veterans with disabilities that 
commenced earlier received greater compensation than those 
with disabilities that commenced later. As the disability is by 
definition a permanent condition, the effect of that payment 
variation is to provide greater compensation to those whose 
disability would likely last longer and whose disability likely 
commenced at an earlier age. The payment formula adopted 
by the Court assigned compensatory amounts for each month 


of disability commencing with January 1, 1970 i 
on December 31, 1994. aaah pista 


The Payment Program eligibility criteria also specifi 
certain guidelines based on the age of the veteran at 9 om 
of disability or death and further contained certain filing 
deadlines by which class members were required to submit 
their claim materials. The Program did not authorize 


payment for deaths or disabilities occurri 
aad tring after the veteran 


276 


Payment levels were established initially based on 
projections regarding the likely volume and characteristics of 
claims. Based upon a review of preliminary claim forms and 
Social Security disability data, the Special Master developed 
initial anticipated claim payment levels. Final payment 
levels were determined after a review of the initial group of 
claims filed. Originally, in the Final Distribution Opinion the 
Court had anticipated that payments to eligible disabled 
veterans might be based on an assigned amount per year of 
disability. The Court noted in that Opinion, however, that 
claims payment formulas might need to be adjusted to 
account for claims experience. Once the initial batch of 
claims was analyzed, the Court was able to finalize a 
payment formula based on actuarial projections regarding the 
anticipated number and nature of unreviewed and future 
claims. Based on the analysis conducted, the Court was able 
to establish a payment amount based on each month of 
disability, which permitted more particularized calculation of 
benefits and a simpler administrative process. The 
calculation formula finally adopted by the Court applied a 
monthly disability payment of $42.67 for each month of 
disability during the Program term. This payment formula 
was maintained throughout the entire period of distribution 
of the Settlement Fund. 

Payments to disabled veterans eligible to reccive a 
disability award under the Payment Program guidelines were 
originally issued in annual installments. Each year the 
claimant was required to re-certify the disability, i.e., confirm 
its continued existence, in order to receive the next 
installment payment. As described below, this installment 
payment system was modified at a later stage in the Program. 


8 The final payment formula approved by the Court resulted in a 
maximum aggregate inability award of $12,800 and a maximum survivor 
payment of $3,400.

eligible survivors to consist of spouses and minor children of 


veterans. As described below, the definition of eligible 
survivor was modified to incorporate other family members.

During the course of implementation of the Payment 
Program, the Court issued periodic orders modifying 
eligibility and claim-filing guidelines. These modifications 
were based on an evaluation of claims experience and were 
adopted in order to respond to claimants’ needs and to 
distribute more efficiently the assets of the Settlement Fund 
Following iS 4 summary of the modifications made to 
specific distribution guidelines:

Initially, the Distribution Opinion provided that 
compensation for deceased veterans be provided only to

3. Modification of Installment Payments. The 
Distribution Opinion required that payments to eligible 
disabled veterans would be made in annual installments. 
The Court directed the provision of a partial lump-sum 


ss Ses? | ~ 


278 


payment to enable eligible veterans to receive a greater 
portion of their total award earlier. This modification 
was deemed possible based on an actuarial analysis of 
claims filings. Order Regarding Modifications to the 
Agent Orange Veteran Payment Program, November 
15, 1989. 

4. Provision of Lump-Sum Payments. The distribution 
plan, as noted, originally called for the payment of 
disability awards in installments and further permitted 
for the adjustment of awards in the event that claims 
experience demonstrated that payment levels should 
either be increased or decreased. During the course of 
the Program implementation, the Special Master 
continued to monitor the payment and claims experience 
to determine whether any adjustment was required. 
During the initial years of the implementation of the 
Payment Program, actuarial analyses were conducted 
every quarter. These analyses reviewed projected claims 
volume and character as compared to claims filings and 
actual claim statistics, and they determined whether, 
based on that information, the Settlement Fund’s assets 
would be sufficient to pay all anticipated claims at the 
payment levels established initially by the Court. In 
later years of the Payment Program, these actuarial 
analyses were supplemented by further estimates 
conducted by Price Waterhouse. In 1993 the Special 
Master reported to the Court that actuarial projections 
indicated that the Payment Program account would have 
sufficient assets to pay all claims filed by the publicized 
closure date at the payment levels initially established by 
the Court. In light of that report, the Court directed that 
all claims should thereafter be paid on a lump-sum basis. 
Commencing in September 1993 all Payment Program 
participants who had previously received an installment 
payment were sent a lump-sum payment constituting the 
unpaid remainder of their total award. Any applicant 


— 


279 


approved for payment after that date received the entire 
award in a lump-sum payment. Order, June 10, 1993.

of requests for application kits from class members. The 
Claims Administrator reported that it was unable to 
respond to all the requests in time to permit the actual 
filing of application kits by the December 31, 1994 
deadline. Based on that report, the Court authorized a 
brief extension — until January 17, 1995 — for class

pool of appropriate candidates capable of providing the 
necessary services in the most cost efficient manner.

Claims were submitted from each state in the 
States plus the District of Columbia and Puerto Rico. A 
small portion of claims were submitted by individuals 
residing outside the United States (including Guam and the 


281 


U.S. Virgin Islands). The states with the highest number of 
claimants were Texas (7,162 claims), California (7,106 
claims), and Florida (5,924 claims). In addition to those 
three states, five other states accounted for over 4,000 claims 
each: Georgia, North Carolina, New York, Ohio and 
Pennsylvania. The states with the smallest number of 
claimants were North Dakota (169 claims), Vermont (169 
claims) and Wyoming (177 claims), and the District of 
Columbia was included in this category as well (161 claims). 
The approval and denial rates vary in each state, and there 
does not appear to be any discernable pattern. A breakdown 
of all claims by state of the claimants’ residences is contained 
in the Final Report of the Claims Administrator. 


The Claims Administrator tracked the general cause of 
the disability or death among the approved claims and, where 
discernable, among the denied claims. The Claims 
Administrator’s statistics indicate 19 different causes of 
death or disability among claimants. The most common 
cause of disability among approved disability claimants was 
mental disorder (including post traumatic stress disorder). 
Heart disease, cancer and diseases of the bones and organs of 
movement were the next most common causes of disability 


_ among approved disability claims. The most common causes 


of death resulting in approved survivor claims were cancer, 
heart disease and diseases of the digestive system — with 
cancer accounting for 59 percent of the approved survivor 
claims.

6. Basis for Ineligibility. orig 
i i luding 
i could be denied for a variety of reasons inc 
sensi any of the required eligibility criteria, failure 


to submit information sufficient to evaluate the claim, and 


283 


failure to file the claim by the Court-mandated deadline. For 
disability claims, by far the most common reason for denial 
of a claim was a finding by the Claims Administrator that the 
applicant had not demonstrated a total disability. A total of 
44 percent of the disability claims denied were denied based 
on the applicants’ failure to meet the disability requirement. 
A determination that the applicant failed to meet the 
disability requirement could be based on evidence that the 
applicant continued substantial employment, or presentation 
of medical evidence showing an illness that was not totally 
disabling. A total of 7 percent of the disability claims denied 
were denied based on a finding that the disability was in fact 
caused by an accident or traumatic injury. Thirteen percent 
of the disability denials resulted from a finding that the 
veteran did not satisfy the Court-approved standard for 
demonstrating a probability of exposure to herbicides based 
on the circumstances and location of service. Most 
commonly, a denial based on exposure would occur where 
the veteran served outside the country (such as on an off- 
Shore Naval vessel) or served very late or very early in the 
war when herbicide spraying was either confined to a limited 
area or had been discontinued. Very few claims were denied 
as untimely: a total of 275 disability claims (less than | 
percent of all disability claim denials) were disallowed based 
on failure to file by the required deadline. 


The most common basis for denial of a survivor claim 
was the failure to satisfy the Court-approved standard for 
exposure. A total of 39 percent of the survivor claims denied 
(13 percent of all survivor claims) were denied based on the 
exposure criterion. A relatively substantial number of claims 
(1,109 or 16 percent of all survivor claim denials) were 
denied on the ground that the veteran was over age 60 at the 
time of death. A large portion of claims (1,303 or 19 percent 
of survivor denials) were denied for multiple reasons.

In re, 
“AGENT ORANGE” MDL No. 381 
(JIBW) 

Product Liability Litigation 

x 

ORDER | 


The Dow Chemical Company has brought on an order to 
show cause asking that the $10 million reserve for 
indemnification of defendants established in the Agent 
Orange class action Settlement Agreement be released to the 
Agent Orange Class Assistance Program for the benefit of 
the class in accordance with the court’s discretion. Based on 
the supporting Affidavit of Steven Brock dated April 27, 
1994, the decisions of this Court and the Court of Appeals in 
In re Agent Orange Product Liability Litigation, 781 F. 
Supp. 902 (E.D.N.Y. 1991), aff'd, 996 F. Supp. 1425 (2d Cir. 
1993), cert. denied, 114 S. Ct. 1125 (1994), and the full 


288 


record in the ongoing indemnity proceedings in MDL 
No.381, the Court concludes that the indemnify reserve 
should immediately be released. 


Indemnification of defendants for state court Agent 
Orange judgments is no longer a realistic possibility. Those 
plaintiffs who originally opted out of the class have either 
had their claims dismissed or have opted back into the class. 
As to class members who did not timely opt out of the class, 
the Court of Appeals has affirmed that this Court retains 
jurisdiction to enforce the Settlement Agreement, including 
in particular its provisions barring relitigation by class 
members. Any further state court suits by class members 
will not result in state court judgments because removal and 
transfer to MDL No. 381 will continue to be available to 
defendants. The Court’s retained jurisdiction to enforce the 
Settlement Agreement, decide questions of class 
membership, and enforce its injunction against relitigation 
will continue even after all settlement funds have been 
distributed. 

While the $10 million fund for indemnification of 
defendants was to be held in reserve for twenty-five years, 
these changed circumstances make it appropriate for the 
Court to exercise the discretion which it has retained over the 
Settlement Fund to release the $10 million reserve to the 
Agent Orange Class Assistance Program. The release of the 
reserve will permit the Class Assistance Program to continue 
to provide efficient and effective assistance to class members 
until its funds are exhausted and its operations concluded. 
The Court continues to retain discretion over the Settlement 
Fund to make indemnification payments to defendants in the 
future in accordance with the Settlement Agreement to the 
extent that funds remain available and the Court determines 
that such indemnification payments would be in the interest 
of justice. Accordingly, it is hereby 

ORDERED that the $10 million indemnity reserve 
provided for defendants in the Settlement Agreement be 


289 


immediately released to the Agent Orange Class Assistance 
Program.

JACK B. WEINSTEIN

AFFIDAVIT OF JOE ISAACSON 
IN OPPOSITION TO DEFENDANTS’ MOTION TO 
DISMISS 


I, Joe Isaacson, being duly sworn, depose and say as 
follows: 


1. I served in Vietnam and am informed and believe that I 
was exposed to phenoxy herbicides, including “Agent 
Orange,” during my tour of duty in Vietnam fron: September 
1968 to September 1969. Among the places that I served 
during this period was Phan Rang Air Force base. 

2. After I returned from Vietnam, I paid little attention to 
whether or not I had been exposed to herbicides, including 
Agent Orange, while serving in Vietnam. 

3. Before 1996, I was unaware that the cases of veterans who 
claimed to have been injured by these herbicides were 
consolidated into a single action in Brooklyn known as In re 
“Agent Orange” Product Liability Litigation nor was I aware 
of the existence of a class action lawsuit on behalf of 
veterans who were exposed to herbicides. 


4. Before 1996, I never filed a lawsuit related to my exposure 
to herbicides in Vietnam, I did not consult with any attorneys 
regarding my exposure to herbicides in Vietnam, I never 
contacted the Veterans Administration or the Department of 
Veterans’ Affairs regarding my herbicide exposure, | 
belonged to no veterans’ organizations, and I was not aware 
(and am still not aware) of being listed on any “Agent 
Orange” registry. 

5. I do not recall ever seeing, reading or hearing any notice of 
a class action for herbicide-exposed Vietnam veterans, 
whether by direct mail or by publication, either in print, on 
radio, or on television. 


292 


6. I do not recall ever receiving any notice of a class which 
included: “[{T]hose persons who were in the United States, 
New Zealand or Australian Armed Forces at any time from 
1961 to 1972 who were injured while in or near Vietnam by 
exposure to Agent Orange or other phenoxy herbicides .... ” 
If I had received this notice in 1983 or 1984, I would not 
have believed it to have included me. This is because it was 
not until 1996, when I was diagnosed with non-Hodgkin’s 
lymphoma, that I believed that I had in any way been injured 
by exposure to ‘Agent Orange or other phenoxy herbicides’ 
while I was in Vietnam. 

7. I do not recall ever receiving any notice of a settlement of 
a class action on behalf of Vietnam veterans exposed to 
‘Agent Orange and other phenoxy herbicides’ at any time. 
Before 1996 I was never advised in any fashion as to what 
effect such a settlement might have on my rights to bring a 
claim in the future. Having not heard of such a settlement, 
before 1996 I certainly do not recall ever being advised of 
any rights to object to the terms of such a settlement or being 
told any particulars of the settlement, nor do I recall being 
advised of a right to opt out of either a class action or a class 
action settlement. 


8. In 1996 I was employed as an assistant principal in a 
Middle School setting. In the late summer of 1996 I was 
diagnosed with non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma. I am informed 
and believe that the Department of Veterans’ Affairs has 
determined that my non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma is connected 
to my service in Vietnam. Before being diagnosed with non- 
Hodgkin’s lymphoma, I did not believe that I had suffered 
any injuries related to my exposure to Agent Orange and 
other phenoxy herbicides in Vietnam. I do believe that my 
non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma is related to my exposure to 
phenoxy herbicides in Vietnam. 

9. It was only after being diagnosed with non-Hodgkin’s 
lymphoma that I became aware of the existence of a class 
action and a class action settlement on behalf of Vietnam 


293 


veterans which took place around 1984. I further became 
aware that at the time of my diagnosis neither I nor my heirs 
would be able to receive any funds from that particular 
settlement, because all settlement funds had been paid out 
without preparing for the fact that I and other veterans might 
get cancer from Agent Orange after 1994. I, therefore, have 
not received any payments from what I understand to have 
been called the Agent Orange Veterans’ Payment Program. 


10. I also am not aware of having received any benefits 
related to my Vietnam ‘Agent Orange and phenoxy 
herbicide’ exposure from any organization funded by what 
has been called the Agent Orange Class Assistance Program. 
11. I therefore am not aware of having received any 
compensation in any form from any corporation that 
manufactured the phenoxy herbicides that I was exposed to 
which caused my cancer. 


I verify under penalties of perjury that the foregoing is true 
and correct. 


/s/_ Joe Isaacson 
Joe Isaacson 


State of New Jersey, County of Ocean, Subscribed and sworn 
to before me on November 21, 1999 by Joe Isaacson. 


Rose Janowski 
November 21, 1999

AFFIDAVIT OF LAURA WELCH 


I, Laura S. Welch, with personal knowledge hereby 
declare and affirm under penalties of perjury the following: 

1. My name is Laura Welch and I am over the age of 
eighteen years. 

2. 1am a medical doctor, certified in occupational and 
environmental medicine. 

3. Ihave held faculty positions at the Albert Einstein 
School of Medicine, Yale University and George Washington 


295 


University. I have published over 50 peer reviewed articles 
or book chapters in the field of occupational and 
environmental medicine. (A true and correct copy of my 
curriculum vitae is attached hereto as Exhibit “A.”’) 


4. I am familiar with the process by which the 
scientific and medical community and governmental and 
quasi-governmental agencies assess the questions about 
whether the exposure to a particular substance has adverse 
human health effects. 


5. I have followed the opinions of the medical and 
scientific community regarding potential health effects to 
Viet Nam veterans of exposure to the phenoxy herbicides 
which were sprayed in Viet Nam, including the 2,4,5-T and 
2,4-D combination known as “Agent Orange.” 


6. During the decade beginning 1975 through 1985, 
the scientific community tried to determine whether exposure 
to these chemicals led to adverse health effects in Viet Nam 
veterans. Much of this analysis relied upon occupational 
studies of herbicide sprayers and manufacturing workers. 

7. At the time it was believed, based upon 
toxicological studies, that “dioxin,” which was a contaminant 
of many of these herbicide combinations was a potent 
carcinogen. (See, for example, Kociba, R.J., D.G. Keyes, 
J.E. Beyer, R.M. Carreon, C.E. Wade, D.A. Dittenber, R.P. 
Kalnins, L.E. Frauson, C.N. Park, S.D. Barnard, R.A. 
Hummel, and C.G. Humiston “ Results of a two-year chronic 
toxicity and oncogenicity study oof  2,3,7,8- 
tetrachlorodibenze-p-dioxin in rats”. Toxicity And Applied 
Pharmacology, 469: 279-303 (1978).) 

8. However, during that time, there was insufficient 
data to evaluate fully the human health effects of such 
herbicide exposure. Because there was very little data and 
what existed was based on small sample sizes and limited 
exposure characterization, as is typical in the early stages of 
these kinds of inquiries, the data available for human effects 
left the question as to whether there were such health effects 


296 


inconclusive with the notable exception of skin condition 
called chloracne. 


9. Before 1984, to the extent that there were any 
human cancers caused by these herbicide combinations, most 
of the studies in existence at the time suggested the 
possibility of a relationship to either soft-tissue sarcomas or 
non-Hodgkin’s lymphomas. (See, for example: Ericksson, 
M., L. Hardell, N.O. Berg, T. Moller, and O. Axelson. 
“Case-control study on malignant mesenchymal tumors of 
the soft tissue and exposure to chemical substance.” 
Lakartidningen 76: 3872-75, 1979; Ericksson, M., “Soft 
tissue sarcomas and exposure to chemical substances: A case 
referent study,” British Journal of Industrial Medicine, 38: 
27-33 (1981); Ericksson M., Hardell L., Berg N.O., Moller 
T., Axelson O., “Soft tissue sarcomas and exposure to 
chemical substances: a case reference study,” Br. J. Indust. 
Med. 38: 27-33 (1981); Honchar, P.A and Halperin, W.E., 
“2 4,5-T, trichlorophenol, and soft tissue sarcoma.” Lancet 
1: 268-269 (1981); Johnson, F.E., Kugler, M. A., Brown, 
S.M., “Soft tissue sarcomas and chlorinated phenols,” Lancet 
2: 40 (1981); Fingerhut, M.A., Halperin, W.E., Honchar, 
P.A., Smith, A.B., Groth, D.H., and Russell, W.O., “An 
evaluation of reports of dioxin exposure and soft tissue 
sarcoma pathology among chemical workers in the United 
States,” Scand. J. Work Environ. Health 10: 299-303 (1984); 
Fingerhut, M.A. and Halperin, W.E. “Dioxin exposure and 
sarcoma,” J. of the Am. Med. Assn. (JAMA) 249: 3176-3177 
(1983); Cantor, K.P. “Farming and Mortality from Non- 
Hodgkin’s Lymphoma: a case-control study,” Int. J. Cancer. 
29: 239-247 (1982); Sarma PR, Jacobs, J. “Thoracic soft- 
tissue sarcoma in Vietnam veterans exposed to Agent 
Orange,” New England J. Med 306 (18) 1109 (1982); 
Bishop, C.M. and Jones, A.H., “Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma of 
the Scalp in Workers exposed to Dioxins,” Lancet 2: 369 
(1981); Hardell, L., Sandstrom, A., “Case-control study: Soft 
tissue sarcomas and exposure to phenoxyactic acids or 
chlorophenols,” Br. J. Cancer 39: 711-717 (1979); Hardell, 


297 


L., Erickson, M., Lenner, P., and Lundgren, E., “Malignant 
lymphoma and exposure to chemicals, especially organic 
solvents, chlorophenols and phenoxy acids: a case-control 
study,” Br. J. Cancer 43: 169-176 (1981); Buesching, D.P. 
and Wollstadt, L., “Cancer Mortality among Farmers. JNCI 
72; 503-504 (1984); Fingerhut, M.A., Halperin, W.E., 
Honchar, P.A., Smith, A.B., Groth, D.H., and Russell, W.O., 
“An evaluation of reports of dioxin exposure and soft tissue 
sarcoma pathology among chemical workers in the United 
States,” Scand. J. Work Environ. Health 10: 299-303 (1984); 
Hardell, L., “Relation of soft-tissue sarcoma, malignant 
lymphoma and colon cancer to phenoxy acids, 
chlorophenols, and other Agents.” Scand. J. Work Environ. 
Health 7: 119-130 (1981); Moses, M. and Selikoff, LJ. “Soft 
tissue sarcomas, phenoxy herbicides, and chlorinated 
phenols,” Lancet 1:1370 (1981); and Ollsson, H. and Brandt, 
L., “Non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma of the skin and occupational 
exposure to herbicides,” The Lancet, p. 579, Sep. 12 (1981).) 

10. However, though suggestive, these studies and 
reports were inadequate to come to scientific conclusions 
regarding the human carcinogencity of the phenoxy 
herbicides used in Viet Nam. The problems with the studies 
varied. For example, if the size of the group is small, a study 
can only detect a large increase in risk. If the exposure 


assessment is not adequate, misclassification of exposure can 
occur. 


11. Subsequently, Pl 98-542 mandated the National 
Academy of Sciences to create a scientific review process 
that began with the National Academy of Sciences and 
outside expert panelists reviewing the large body of scientific 
literature in order to assess scientific knowledge regarding 
the health effects of the herbicides sprayed in Viet Nam. 


12. The resulting report of the National Academy of 
Sciences’ Institute of Medicine includes a rigorous peer 
reviewed investigation of all scientific data, weighing the 
strength and validity of such data. The results of this process 


298 


are reported in two reports entitled Veterans and Agent 
Orange, one dated 1994 and the other dated in 1996. Current 
information about this project and the drafts of the latest 
study are available on the Internet site of the National 
Academy of Sciences. 


13. The conclusion of the reports of the Institute of 
Medicine of the National Academy of Sciences indicate that 
there are eleven diseases that have been concluded by the 
scientific literature to be causally related to phenoxy 
herbicide exposure. These different diseases are each 
discussed in the reports and the strength of the data regarding 
each disease is presented. Non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma is in 
the category of those diseases for which the scientific 
literature has the strongest association between the disease 
and phenoxy herbicide exposure. 

14. These reports show very powerfully that the 
scientific community today has a very much more advanced 
view regarding the diseases caused by phenoxy herbicide 
exposure than a decade ago. At the time that the original 
litigation in In RE: Agent Orange took, it was a credible 
scientific position to state that other than chloracne there 
were few, if any, diseases for which there was sufficient, 
widely accepted evidence in the scientific community, 
demonstrating that phenoxy herbicides were a causative 
agent. At this point, because we have much more 
information, the scientific view has reversed 180 degrees and 
there is a high degree of consensus that certain phenoxy 
herbicide exposures is related to cancer of particular types, 
such as non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma and soft-tissue sarcomas, 
among others. 

15. The development of knowledge decades after 
exposure is typical for diseases for which there is a latency 
period, including virtually all types of cancer. Because of the 
latency period that exists between exposure to a hazardous 
substance and the onset of cancer, it can take decades before 
health effects occur, and even longer before they are 


id 


299 


observable in study populations. The concept of latency for 
carcinogenesis and the fact that this could take decades — 
often in excess of thirty years — has been known since well 
before 1984. For instance, there are many other such 
exposure-response relationships that took years to occur and 
even more years to become apparent because of latency 
periods, including the links between asbestos and 
mesothelioma, benzene and leukemia, and even tobacco and 
lung cancer. 


LAURA WELCH

Foreword 


In response to decades of concern surrounding the 
possible long-term health consequences of exposures to 
herbicides and the contaminant dioxin. Congress directed the 
Secretary of Veterans Affairs, in Public Law 102-4 signed on 
February 6, 1991, to request the National Academy of 
Sciences (NAS) to conduct a comprehensive review and 
evaluation of the available scientific and medical information 
regarding the health effects of exposure to Agent Orange and 
other herbicides used during the Vietnam conflict. This 
report from the Institute of Medicine (IOM). Committee to 
Review the Health Effects in Vietnam Veterans of Exposure 
to Herbicides is hereby submitted in compliance with Public 
Law 102-4. 

Veterans and Agent Orange: Health Effects of 
Herbicides Used in Vietnam reviews and evaluates the 
available scientific evidence regarding the association 
between exposure to dioxin or other chemical compounds in 
herbicides used in Vietnam and a wide range of health effects 
and provides the committee’s best assessment of this body of 
knowledge for the Secretary of Veterans Affairs to consider 
as the Department of Veterans Affairs exercises its 
responsibilities to Vietnam veterans. The report also 
describes areas in which the available scientific data are 
insufficient to determine whether an association exists and 
provides the committee’s recommendations for areas in 
which future research is likely to be most productive. 

That Congress would ask the NAS -- a nongovernmental 
organization -- to conduct this study reflects a time-honored 
tradition. Created by an act of Congress and signed into law 
in 1863 by President Abraham Lincoln, the NAS is dedicated 
to the furtherance of science and technology and to their 


321 


TABLE 1-1 Summary of Findings in Occupational, 
Environmental, and Veterans Studies Regarding the 
Association Between Specific Health Problems and Exposure 
to Herbicides 


Sufficient Evidence of an Association 
Evidence is sufficient to conclude that there is a positive 

association. That is, a positive association has been observed 
between herbicides and the outcome in studies in which 
chance, bias, and confounding could be ruled out with 
reasonable confidence. For example, if several small studies 
that are free from bias and confounding show an association 
that is consistent to magnitude and directive, there may be 
sufficient evidence for an association. There is sufficient 
evidence of an association between exposure to herbicides 
and the following health outcomes: 

Soft tissue sarcoma 

Non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma 

Hodgkin’s disease 

Chloracne 

Porphyria cutanea tarda (in genetically 

susceptible individuals) 


Limited/Suggestive Evidence of an Association 

Evidence is suggestive of an association beiween 
herbicides and the outcome but is limited because chance, 
bias, and confounding could not be ruled out with 
confidence. For example, at least one high-quality study 
shows a positive association, but the results of other studies 
are inconsistent. There is limited/suggestive evidence of an 
associaiion between exposure to herbicides and the following 
health outcomes: 


Respiratory cancers (lung, larynx, trachea) 
Prostate cancer 


Multiple myeloma 


322 


Inadequate/Insufficient Evidence to Determine Whether 
an Association Exists 
The available studies are of insufficient quality, 

consistency, or statistical power to permit a conclusion 
regarding the presence or absence of an association. For 
example, studies fail to control for confounding, have 
inadequate exposure assessment, or fai! to address latency. 
There is inadequate or insufficient evidence to determine 
whether an association exists between exposure to herbicides 
and the following health outcomes: 

Hepatobiliary cancers 

Nasal/nasopharyngeal cancer 

Bone cancer 

Female reproductive cancers (cervical, 

uterine, ovarian) and breast cancer 

Renal cancer 

Testicular cancer 

Leukemia 

Spontaneous abortion 

Birth defects 

Neonatal/infant death and stillbirths 

Low birthweight 

Childhood cancer in offspring

chance, bias, and confounding could be ruled out with 
reasonable confidence. For example, if several small studies

AMENDED NOTICE OF APPEAL 

Daniel Raymond Stephenson, Susan Stephenson, Daniel 
Anthony Stephenson and Emily Stephenson are the originally 
named plaintiffs in the above captioned matter. The U.S. 
District Court for the Eastern District of New York 
incorrectly lodged this matter when it was transferred to the 
MDL 381 action from the District Court for the Western 
District of Louisiana on June 1, 1999. On April 7, 2000 the 
Notice of Appeal was filed with the U.S. District Court for 
the Eastern District of New York and the caption failed to 
indicate more than one named plaintiff. 

Therefore, Daniel Raymond Stephenson, Susan 
Stephenson, Daniel Anthony Stephenson and Emily 
Stephenson amends their Notice of Appeal previously filed 
by correcting the caption to include all originally named 
plaintiffs in their appeal to the United States Court of 
Appeals for the Second Circuit from the final judgment 
entered in this action on the 27" day of March, 2000.

ORDER AND JUDGMENT 


The Court heard argument on defendants’ motion to 
dismiss in the above-captioned actions on December 13, 
1999. A copy of the transcript of proceedings is attached 
hereto. Upon consideration of that argument and all 
pleadings, motions, proceedings, and the full record in MDL- 
381 and ir ihe above-captioned actions, it is hereby 

ORDFRED that defendants’ motion to dismiss is 
GRANTED, and 


IT IS FURTHER ORDERED that the complaints and 
any and all amendments thereto filed in /saacson v. Dow 
Chemical Company, et al, Civ. No. 98-6383 (JBW) and 
Stephenson v. Dow Chemical Company et al., Civ. No. 99- 
3056 (JBW), shall be dismissed as against all defendants 
with prejudice and without costs assessed to any party.
